    "oldString": "Hybrid linear recurrent architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2, GLA, DeltaNet) achieve aggregate benchmark scores within 1--2 points of each other",
    "newString": "Hybrid linear recurrent architectures \\citep[GatedDeltaNet,][]{yang2024gateddeltanet}, \\citep[RetNet,][]{sun2023retnet}, \\citep[HGRN2,][]{qin2024hgrn2}, \\citep[GLA,][]{yang2024gla}, \\citep[DeltaNet;][]{yang2024deltanet}) achieve aggregate benchmark scores within 1--2 points of each other",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHybrid linear recurrent architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2, GLA, DeltaNet) achieve aggregate benchmark scores within 1--2 points of each other at matched parameter budgets, yet their helped and hurt example sets overlap by only ${\\sim}10$\\% (pairwise Jaccard $\\approx 0.10$). This gap between aggregate equivalence and per-example behavioral equivalence is invisible in standard metrics: architectures that score similarly reach that score by gaining and losing on different inputs, and the choice of backbone carries hidden consequences for which specific examples a deployed system handles correctly.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.56\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}%\n\\hfill\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.42\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig4_jaccard_saturation.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}\n\\caption{Per-example overlap appears stable from minimal attention across benchmarks. (a,\\,b) LAMBADA: cross-architecture $\\sigma$ shows a non-significant decreasing trend at 1.3B (solid; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed), while pairwise Jaccard of helped/hurt sets appears stable from 4\\% attention onward. (c) This pattern holds across all four main benchmarks: mean pairwise $\\bar{J}$ remains flat across attention ratios (all $\\rho \\leq 0.80$, $p \\geq .333$). Shaded bands are illustrative ($n = 3$).}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nPrior work on predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} and underspecification \\citep{damour2022underspecification,mccoy2020berts} documents this risk for seed variation within a single architecture, but whether it extends to architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models remains open. If architectures that score similarly in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, the backbone choice carries hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first controlled within-family per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0--25\\%) and two scales (340M, 1.3B). We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed categories. On four benchmarks (LAMBADA, $N = 5{,}153$; HellaSwag, $N = 10{,}042$; PIQA, $N = 1{,}838$; ARC-Easy, $N = 2{,}376$) we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap via Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central finding: architectures that appear equivalent by aggregate benchmarks diverge systematically at the per-example level, and this divergence is established by minimal attention.\n\nOur central finding is that architectural variation produces structured predictive multiplicity whose magnitude depends on task type, not attention ratio. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), varies $2\\times$ across task types but only 0.03 within each benchmark across attention ratios; pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across all ten five-architecture pairs). A supporting observation is that per-example overlap appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat at higher ratios. Even on LAMBADA, where aggregate variance shows a non-significant decreasing trend ($\\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; at 1.3B only), per-example Jaccard does not change (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}).\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item Architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models, controlled within a single model zoo sharing training data and hyperparameters, produces structured predictive multiplicity whose magnitude varies by task type (or evaluation format) rather than attention ratio (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}). Five-architecture validation confirms the pattern (\\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}; Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_arch}).\n    \\item Per-example overlap appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\\%) across all four benchmarks: minimal attention may establish which examples each architecture affects, with no observed homogenization at higher ratios ($n = 5$ ratios; \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}).\n    \\item Pure-model confidence margins partially predict which examples attention will affect (AUC 0.70--0.74), with rescue thresholds architecture-invariant but disruption thresholds varying 47\\% across architectures, constituting a second dimension of multiplicity beyond per-example disagreement (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}; Appendix~\\ref{app:prediction_framework}).\n\\end{itemize}\n\\noindent Code and data are available at \\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/entrainment_gated_ft}.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHybrid linear recurrent architectures \\citep[GatedDeltaNet,][]{yang2024gateddeltanet}, \\citep[RetNet,][]{sun2023retnet}, \\citep[HGRN2,][]{qin2024hgrn2}, \\citep[GLA,][]{yang2024gla}, \\citep[DeltaNet;][]{yang2024deltanet}) achieve aggregate benchmark scores within 1--2 points of each other at matched parameter budgets, yet their helped and hurt example sets overlap by only ${\\sim}10$\\% (pairwise Jaccard $\\approx 0.10$). This gap between aggregate equivalence and per-example behavioral equivalence is invisible in standard metrics: architectures that score similarly reach that score by gaining and losing on different inputs, and the choice of backbone carries hidden consequences for which specific examples a deployed system handles correctly.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.56\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}%\n\\hfill\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.42\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig4_jaccard_saturation.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}\n\\caption{Per-example overlap appears stable from minimal attention across benchmarks. (a,\\,b) LAMBADA: cross-architecture $\\sigma$ shows a non-significant decreasing trend at 1.3B (solid; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed), while pairwise Jaccard of helped/hurt sets appears stable from 4\\% attention onward. (c) This pattern holds across all four main benchmarks: mean pairwise $\\bar{J}$ remains flat across attention ratios (all $\\rho \\leq 0.80$, $p \\geq .333$). Shaded bands are illustrative ($n = 3$).}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nPrior work on predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} and underspecification \\citep{damour2022underspecification,mccoy2020berts} documents this risk for seed variation within a single architecture, but whether it extends to architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models remains open. If architectures that score similarly in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, the backbone choice carries hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first controlled within-family per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0--25\\%) and two scales (340M, 1.3B). We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed categories. On four benchmarks (LAMBADA, $N = 5{,}153$; HellaSwag, $N = 10{,}042$; PIQA, $N = 1{,}838$; ARC-Easy, $N = 2{,}376$) we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap via Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central finding: architectures that appear equivalent by aggregate benchmarks diverge systematically at the per-example level, and this divergence is established by minimal attention.\n\nOur central finding is that architectural variation produces structured predictive multiplicity whose magnitude depends on task type, not attention ratio. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), varies $2\\times$ across task types but only 0.03 within each benchmark across attention ratios; pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across all ten five-architecture pairs). A supporting observation is that per-example overlap appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat at higher ratios. Even on LAMBADA, where aggregate variance shows a non-significant decreasing trend ($\\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; at 1.3B only), per-example Jaccard does not change (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}).\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item Architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models, controlled within a single model zoo sharing training data and hyperparameters, produces structured predictive multiplicity whose magnitude varies by task type (or evaluation format) rather than attention ratio (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}). Five-architecture validation confirms the pattern (\\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}; Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_arch}).\n    \\item Per-example overlap appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\\%) across all four benchmarks: minimal attention may establish which examples each architecture affects, with no observed homogenization at higher ratios ($n = 5$ ratios; \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}).\n    \\item Pure-model confidence margins partially predict which examples attention will affect (AUC 0.70--0.74), with rescue thresholds architecture-invariant but disruption thresholds varying 47\\% across architectures, constituting a second dimension of multiplicity beyond per-example disagreement (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}; Appendix~\\ref{app:prediction_framework}).\n\\end{itemize}\n\\noindent Code and data are available at \\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/entrainment_gated_ft}.\n",

On four benchmarks (LAMBADA, $N = 5{,}153$; HellaSwag, $N = 10{,}042$; PIQA, $N = 1{,}838$; ARC-Easy, $N = 2{,}376$) we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap via Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central finding: architectures that appear equivalent by aggregate benchmarks diverge systematically at the per-example level, and this divergence is established by minimal attention.\n\nOur central finding is that architectural variation produces structured predictive multiplicity whose magnitude depends on task type, not attention ratio. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), varies $2\\times$ across task types but only 0.03 within each benchmark across attention ratios; pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across all ten five-architecture pairs). A supporting observation is that per-example overlap appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat at higher ratios. Even on LAMBADA, where aggregate variance shows a non-significant decreasing trend ($\\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; at 1.3B only), per-example Jaccard does not change (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHybrid linear recurrent architectures (GatedDeltaNet \\citealp{yang2024gateddeltanet}, RetNet \\citealp{sun2023retnet}, HGRN2 \\citealp{qin2024hgrn2}, GLA \\citealp{yang2024gla}, DeltaNet \\citealp{yang2024deltanet}) achieve aggregate benchmark scores within 1--2 points of each other at matched parameter budgets, yet their helped and hurt example sets overlap by only ${\\sim}10$\\% (pairwise Jaccard $\\approx 0.10$). This gap between aggregate equivalence and per-example behavioral equivalence is invisible in standard metrics: architectures that score similarly reach that score by gaining and losing on different inputs, and the choice of backbone carries hidden consequences for which specific examples a deployed system handles correctly.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.56\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}%\n\\hfill\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.42\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig4_jaccard_saturation.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}\n\\caption{Per-example overlap appears stable from minimal attention across benchmarks. (a,\\,b) LAMBADA: cross-architecture $\\sigma$ shows a non-significant decreasing trend at 1.3B (solid; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed), while pairwise Jaccard of helped/hurt sets appears stable from 4\\% attention onward. (c) This pattern holds across all four main benchmarks: mean pairwise $\\bar{J}$ remains flat across attention ratios (all $\\rho \\leq 0.80$, $p \\geq .333$). Shaded bands are illustrative ($n = 3$).}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nPrior work on predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} and underspecification \\citep{damour2022underspecification,mccoy2020berts} documents this risk for seed variation within a single architecture, but whether it extends to architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models remains open. If architectures that score similarly in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, the backbone choice carries hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first controlled within-family per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0--25\\%) and two scales (340M, 1.3B). We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed categories. On four benchmarks (LAMBADA, $N = 5{,}153$; HellaSwag, $N = 10{,}042$; PIQA, $N = 1{,}838$; ARC-Easy, $N = 2{,}376$) we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap via Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central finding: architectures that appear equivalent by aggregate benchmarks diverge systematically at the per-example level, and this divergence is established by minimal attention.\n\nOur central finding is that architectural variation produces structured predictive multiplicity whose magnitude depends on task type, not attention ratio. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), varies $2\\times$ across task types but only 0.03 within each benchmark across attention ratios; pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across all ten five-architecture pairs). A supporting observation is that per-example overlap appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat at higher ratios. Even on LAMBADA, where aggregate variance shows a non-significant decreasing trend ($\\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; at 1.3B only), per-example Jaccard does not change (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}).\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item Architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models, controlled within a single model zoo sharing training data and hyperparameters, produces structured predictive multiplicity whose magnitude varies by task type (or evaluation format) rather than attention ratio (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}). Five-architecture validation confirms the pattern (\\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}; Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_arch}).\n    \\item Per-example overlap appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\\%) across all four benchmarks: minimal attention may establish which examples each architecture affects, with no observed homogenization at higher ratios ($n = 5$ ratios; \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}).\n    \\item Pure-model confidence margins partially predict which examples attention will affect (AUC 0.70--0.74), with rescue thresholds architecture-invariant but disruption thresholds varying 47\\% across architectures, constituting a second dimension of multiplicity beyond per-example disagreement (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}; Appendix~\\ref{app:prediction_framework}).\n\\end{itemize}\n\\noindent Code and data are available at \\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/entrainment_gated_ft}.\n",

```text
40	
41	\subsection{Per-Example Divergence}\label{ssec:per_example}
42	
43	Do architectures converge by improving on the same examples? We compare each architecture's pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid on 5{,}153 LAMBADA examples, classifying each as helped, hurt, or unchanged. Although the decomposition is binary, it captures genuine separation in the continuous confidence space (Cohen's $d = 0.73$--$0.92$ between helped and hurt on LAMBADA; cross-benchmark details in Appendix~\ref{app:cm_cross}). Binarization discards confidence magnitude, so we supplement with continuous confidence margin analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:confidence_margin}) and verify that low Jaccard persists under continuous thresholds (Appendix~\ref{app:continuous_jaccard}).
44	
45	Each architecture displays a distinct profile of per-example changes (Figure~\ref{fig:per_example_bars}). GDN loses a net 153 examples (462 helped, 615 hurt), RetNet loses 69 (426/495), and HGRN2 gains 153 (548/395; full counts in Appendix~\ref{app:per_example_counts}). HGRN2 is the only architecture for which adding attention yields a net per-example benefit. Pairwise Jaccard similarity is low: mean $\bar{J} \leq 0.10$, indicating ${\sim}90$\% non-overlap. Overlap significantly exceeds the independence baseline ($J_{\text{null}} \approx 0.05$, all $z > 5.9$, $p < .0001$; Appendix~\ref{app:jaccard_permutation}), plausibly reflecting shared sensitivity to example difficulty. Normalizing by $J_{\max}$, observed overlap reaches only 6--7\% of the achievable range above chance (Appendix~\ref{app:normalized_es}), confirming that low Jaccard cannot be attributed to set-size constraints.
46	
47	\begin{figure}[t]
48	\centering
49	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_alpha_routing.pdf}
50	\caption{(a) Per-example ambiguity $\alpha$ varies $2\times$ across task types. (b) Confidence-based routing gains track $\alpha$: effective when multiplicity is high (LAMBADA) but counterproductive when low (PIQA). 1.3B, h3-1.}
51	\label{fig:alpha_routing}
52	\end{figure}
53	
54	\paragraph{Confidence margin predictor.} Pure-model confidence on the target token predicts which examples will be affected (AUC 0.70--0.74, a non-parametric measure equivalent to Mann-Whitney $U$; Appendix~\ref{app:confidence_margin}): rescue thresholds are architecture-invariant (1\% spread) but disruption thresholds vary 47\%, explaining cross-architecture Jaccard divergence (Appendix~\ref{app:prediction_framework}). Calibration differences account for less than half the threshold spread (Appendix~\ref{app:calibration}). The rescue pattern generalizes across three benchmarks ($d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\ref{app:cm_cross}); disruption is weaker on reasoning tasks (Appendix~\ref{app:boolq}). This structured disagreement constitutes predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive} under architectural variation.
55	
56	\paragraph{Confidence-based routing.} As an illustrative case study, the rescue-threshold asymmetry suggests a routing strategy: select the architecture whose pure-model confidence best predicts successful rescue. On LAMBADA, this outperforms majority vote by $+5.3$\,pp (SD $= 0.4$\,pp, 10 cross-validated splits), extracting 36\% of the oracle gap (oracle = 65.5\%; Appendix~\ref{app:complementarity}). On ARC-Easy, gains are modest ($+1.0$\,pp, SD $= 0.5$\,pp); on HellaSwag, routing provides no benefit ($-0.02$\,pp, $p = .89$); on PIQA, it is significantly negative ($-0.6$\,pp, $p = .006$). Routing works when multiplicity is high ($\alpha = 0.30$, LAMBADA) and a clear rescue-threshold signal exists, but is counterproductive when multiplicity is low ($\alpha = 0.14$, PIQA). A natural comparison (average-logits ensemble) is left to future work as it requires simultaneous inference across all architectures.
57	
58	\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\alpha$ \citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy; Appendix~\ref{app:arceasy}) to 0.30 (LAMBADA). Format does not drive this ordering: HellaSwag (four-way) and PIQA (two-choice) produce nearly identical $\alpha$ despite different answer-space sizes, and $\alpha$ does not correlate with task accuracy ($\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). This within-multiple-choice invariance suggests that per-example multiplicity is associated with the cognitive demands of the task rather than the mechanical constraints of the evaluation format, though the confound between task type and format cannot be fully resolved with the present benchmarks (Limitations). Bootstrap 95\% CIs confirm the ordering (all non-overlapping except PIQA--HellaSwag; Table~\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\bar{J} \leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many are affected. Within each benchmark, $\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$) but differs $2\times$ across task types (or evaluation formats), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention ratio \citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}.
59	
60	\begin{table}[t]
61	\centering
62	\small
63	\caption{Predictive multiplicity at 1.3B. $\alpha$: ambiguity at h3-1 \citep{marx2020predictive}. $\bar{J}$: mean pairwise Jaccard of helped~(h) and hurt~(u) sets, averaged across four hybrid attention ratios and three architecture pairs. $\rho_\alpha$: Spearman $\rho$ between attention ratio and $\alpha$. Benchmarks ordered by $\alpha$.}
64	\label{tab:multiplicity}
65	\begin{tabular}{@{}lrcccc@{}}
66	\toprule
67	 & $N$ & $\alpha$ & $\bar{J}_{h}$ & $\bar{J}_{u}$ & $\rho_\alpha$ \\
68	\midrule
69	PIQA & 1838 & .14 & .091 & .094 & $0.0$ \\

Calibration differences account for less than half the threshold spread (Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}). The rescue pattern generalizes across three benchmarks ($d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cm_cross}); disruption is weaker on reasoning tasks (Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}). This structured disagreement constitutes predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} under architectural variation.\n\n\\paragraph{Confidence-based routing.} As an illustrative case study, the rescue-threshold asymmetry suggests a routing strategy: select the architecture whose pure-model confidence best predicts successful rescue. On LAMBADA, this outperforms majority vote by $+5.3$\\,pp (SD $= 0.4$\\,pp, 10 cross-validated splits), extracting 36\\% of the oracle gap (oracle = 65.5\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:complementarity}). On ARC-Easy, gains are modest ($+1.0$\\,pp, SD $= 0.5$\\,pp); on HellaSwag, routing provides no benefit ($-0.02$\\,pp, $p = .89$); on PIQA, it is significantly negative ($-0.6$\\,pp, $p = .006$). Routing works when multiplicity is high ($\\alpha = 0.30$, LAMBADA) and a clear rescue-threshold signal exists, but is counterproductive when multiplicity is low ($\\alpha = 0.14$, PIQA). A natural comparison (average-logits ensemble) is left to future work as it requires simultaneous inference across all architectures.\n\n\\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy; Appendix~\\ref{app:arceasy}) to 0.30 (LAMBADA). Format does not drive this ordering: HellaSwag (four-way) and PIQA (two-choice) produce nearly identical $\\alpha$ despite different answer-space sizes, and $\\alpha$ does not correlate with task accuracy ($\\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). This within-multiple-choice invariance suggests that per-example multiplicity is associated with the cognitive demands of the task rather than the mechanical constraints of the evaluation format, though the confound between task type and format cannot be fully resolved with the present benchmarks (Limitations). Bootstrap 95\\% CIs confirm the ordering (all non-overlapping except PIQA--HellaSwag; Table~\\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\bar{J} \\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many are affected. Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$) but differs $2\\times$ across task types (or evaluation formats), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention ratio \\citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Predictive multiplicity at 1.3B. $\\alpha$: ambiguity at h3-1 \\citep{marx2020predictive}. $\\bar{J}$: mean pairwise Jaccard of helped~(h) and hurt~(u) sets, averaged across four hybrid attention ratios and three architecture pairs. $\\rho_\\alpha$: Spearman $\\rho$ between attention ratio and $\\alpha$. Benchmarks ordered by $\\alpha$.}\n\\label{tab:multiplicity}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n & $N$ & $\\alpha$ & $\\bar{J}_{h}$ & $\\bar{J}_{u}$ & $\\rho_\\alpha$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPIQA & 1838 & .14 & .091 & .094 & $0.0$ \\\\",

```text
95	\end{figure*}
96	
97	Each architecture's per-example response to attention is largely idiosyncratic. The observed $\bar{J}$ reaches only 6--7\% of the achievable range above chance ($J_{\max} \approx 0.78$--$0.92$), ruling out ceiling constraints (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}). Readers can verify $\rho$ directly from Table~\ref{tab:convergence}; with $n = 3$ architectures, all bootstrap CIs are heuristic indicators of direction rather than precise coverage.
98	
99	This pattern generalizes beyond LAMBADA (Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling}c): on all four main benchmarks, mean pairwise Jaccard shows no significant trend with attention ratio (all $\rho \leq 0.80$, $p \geq 0.333$), with values uniformly between 0.08 and 0.11 (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_bench_jaccard}).
100	
101	Heuristic bootstrap 95\% CIs ($n = 3$; \S\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}) show non-overlapping $\sigma$ between pure and h3-1 but completely overlapping Jaccard across all hybrid ratios (\S\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}). Figure~\ref{fig:jaccard_hierarchy} shows a three-tier Jaccard hierarchy: independence baseline (${\sim}0.05$), cross-architecture (${\sim}0.10$, $2\times$ baseline), and within-architecture (${\sim}0.33$, $3\times$ cross-architecture; Appendix~\ref{app:within_arch_stability}). Pythia 70M seed calibration yields a comparable within-seed level ($J = 0.27$--$0.39$; Appendix~\ref{app:seed_calibration}), consistent with architecture-associated divergence, though matched multi-seed experiments would be needed for definitive evidence.
102	
103	\begin{figure}[t]
104	\centering
105	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_jaccard_hierarchy.pdf}
106	\caption{Jaccard hierarchy on LAMBADA (1.3B). Cross-architecture overlap ($\bar{J} \approx 0.09$) is $2\times$ the independence baseline but only one-third of within-architecture overlap ($\bar{J} \approx 0.33$). Pythia 70M within-seed Jaccard (different scale) falls at a comparable level. Error bars show the helped--hurt range.}
107	\label{fig:jaccard_hierarchy}
108	\end{figure}
109	
110	Two interpretations are consistent with the observed stability. \textbf{(a) Fingerprint view:} each recurrent backbone imposes a characteristic inductive bias that determines which examples attention can rescue, locking in architecture-specific sensitivity patterns. \textbf{(b) Convergence view:} even minimal attention causes all architectures to rapidly adopt Transformer-like behavior, with residual per-example divergence arising from incomplete convergence. Appendix~\ref{app:transformer_entrainment} shows that h3-1 correct sets indeed shift toward the Transformer baseline, providing partial support for interpretation (b). Disentangling these accounts requires ablation of the attention mechanism itself, for example replacing learned attention weights with random projections. Attention weight analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:attention_patterns}) shows GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions (cosine 0.995) yet differ in per-example effects, suggesting these originate in the recurrent layers. The four-benchmark variation (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}) is consistent with independence: LAMBADA ($\alpha = 0.30$) is the only benchmark showing a variance-reduction trend despite having the highest multiplicity, while PIQA ($\alpha = 0.14$) and HellaSwag ($\alpha = 0.15$) show no variance reduction with the lowest multiplicity.
111	
112	Cross-scale analysis reveals a clear dissociation: per-example Jaccard is consistent across both scales ($\bar{J} = 0.08$--$0.10$ at 340M and 1.3B), whereas the aggregate variance trend is observed only at 1.3B on LAMBADA ($\rho = -0.50$, $p = .45$ at 340M). Ambiguity $\alpha$ is systematically higher at 340M ($\Delta \leq 8$\,pp), suggesting that the \emph{pattern} of per-example multiplicity is stable across scales while its magnitude tracks model capacity (Appendices~\ref{app:scale_convergence}, \ref{app:scale_per_example}--\ref{app:cross_scale}).
113	\vspace{-2pt}
114	\subsection{Robustness}\label{ssec:robustness}
115	
116	Removing GDN weakens the LAMBADA variance reduction ($\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$) and reverses MMLU's ($\rho = +0.6$), both driven by GDN's 16-head configuration (vs.\ 32 in others; Appendix~\ref{app:gdn_confound}). The RetNet--HGRN2 pair preserves the independence pattern ($\sigma \leq 0.008$, $J = 0.086$--$0.108$). The aggregate variance pattern therefore depends on GDN inclusion; the per-example stability pattern does not. Five-architecture validation at 340M (adding GLA and DeltaNet) confirms $J \leq 0.14$ across all ten pairwise comparisons and four benchmarks (all $p < .01$; Figure~\ref{fig:jaccard_heatmap_5arch}; Appendix~\ref{app:extended_arch}), extending the per-example divergence finding to architectures not used in the primary analysis. Full sensitivity analyses appear in Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}; Transformer baseline comparison in Appendix~\ref{app:transformer_entrainment}.
117

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_016wg9H4SsL1aYRpAmL3WiPG`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Method}\label{sec:method}
2	
3	We describe the model configurations, evaluation benchmarks, and analytical framework used to measure how attention affects cross-architecture agreement at both aggregate and per-example levels.
4	
5	\begin{table}[t]
6	\centering
7	\small
8	\caption{Model zoo summary \citep{wang2025hybrid}. All configurations share training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters; they differ only in backbone and attention ratio.}
9	\label{tab:model_zoo}
10	\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
11	\toprule
12	Architecture & Recurrence mechanism & Scales \\
13	\midrule
14	GatedDeltaNet & Gated delta rules & 340M, 1.3B \\
15	RetNet & Multi-scale exp.\ decay & 340M, 1.3B \\
16	HGRN2 & Gated state expansion & 340M, 1.3B \\
17	\midrule
18	\multicolumn{3}{@{}l@{}}{\footnotesize Attention ratios: 0\%, 4\%, 7.7\%, 14.3\%, 25\%} \\
19	\multicolumn{3}{@{}l@{}}{\footnotesize Extended (340M only): +GLA, +DeltaNet} \\
20	\bottomrule
21	\end{tabular}
22	\end{table}
23	
24	\subsection{Model Zoo}\label{ssec:model_zoo}
25	
26	We use the pretrained model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid} (Table~\ref{tab:model_zoo}), which provides controlled comparisons: GatedDeltaNet \citep{yang2024gateddeltanet}, RetNet \citep{sun2023retnet}, and HGRN2 \citep{qin2024hgrn2} at five attention ratios and two scales. The GDN 1.3B h3-1 configuration uses 16 attention heads vs.\ 32 for RetNet and HGRN2 (\S\ref{ssec:robustness}). We adopt the h$k$-1 notation: h3-1 = 25\% attention, h6-1 = 14.3\%, h12-1 = 7.7\%, h24-1 = 4\%. Within each scale, all models share identical training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters, isolating architecture and attention configuration. The zoo also includes a Transformer baseline at both scales.
27	
28	\subsection{Task Taxonomy}\label{ssec:task_taxonomy}
29	
30	We evaluate on 10 benchmarks grouped into four categories defined by the primary cognitive demand of each task. This taxonomy (informal; not pre-registered) was fixed prior to analysis following standard categorizations in the evaluation literature \citep{arora2024zoology} and is treated as hypothesis-generating rather than confirmatory (see Appendix~\ref{app:benchmark_details} for the confirmatory/exploratory split), not derived post hoc from observed results. The categories test whether the effect of attention on cross-architecture variance depends on task type.
31	
32	\begin{itemize}
33	    \item \textbf{Retrieval}: LAMBADA \citep{paperno2016lambada}, which requires predicting a final word from long-range context without reasoning or world knowledge.
34	    \item \textbf{Comprehension}: Boolean Questions (BoolQ), a reading comprehension task with yes/no answers.
35	    \item \textbf{Reasoning}: HellaSwag, AI2 Reasoning Challenge (ARC-Challenge; \citealt{clark2018arc}), WinoGrande, and Physical Intuition Question Answering (PIQA). Choice of Plausible Alternatives (COPA; $n = 100$) is supplementary; its small sample renders $\alpha$ unreliable (Appendix~\ref{app:subsample_sensitivity}).
36	    \item \textbf{Mixed}: ARC-Easy, Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU), SciQ, and XStoryCloze, which combine retrieval, comprehension, and reasoning demands.
37	\end{itemize}
38	
39	LAMBADA is the only free-form generation benchmark in our evaluation; all others use multiple-choice scoring. Its higher $\alpha$ (0.30 vs.\ 0.14--0.21 for multiple-choice tasks) may partly reflect the larger answer space inherent to open-ended prediction, and the multiple-choice format may mechanically constrain per-example divergence by limiting the number of distinct error patterns. We cannot fully disentangle task type from evaluation format with the present benchmarks. However, within multiple-choice tasks, format (two-way vs.\ four-way) does not predict $\alpha$ (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}), suggesting that format alone does not drive the observed variation.
40	
41	All evaluations use lm-evaluation-harness v0.4.x \citep{gao2024lmeval} with default prompts and greedy decoding; accuracy serves as the primary metric for all benchmarks. Bootstrap analyses use random seed 42 throughout.
42	
43	\begin{figure}[t]
44	\centering
45	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_method_overview.pdf}
46	\caption{Experimental framework. For each architecture, we compare the pure-recurrent baseline against its hybrid (+attention) variant, classifying every evaluation example as helped, hurt, or unchanged. Cross-architecture overlap of these per-example effect sets is measured via pairwise Jaccard similarity ($J \approx 0.10$), revealing architecture-specific sensitivity patterns.}
47	\label{fig:method_overview}
48	\end{figure}
49	
50	\subsection{Convergence Metrics}\label{ssec:convergence_metrics}
51	
52	We quantify cross-architecture agreement as a function of attention ratio. For each benchmark and attention ratio, we compute the cross-architecture standard deviation $\sigma$ over the three architectures' accuracy scores. To measure the relationship between attention and convergence, we compute the Spearman rank correlation $\rho$ between attention ratio rank and $\sigma$ across the five attention configurations ($n = 5$). A negative $\rho$ indicates that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.
53	
54	We also report the convergence ratio $\sigma_{\text{pure}} / \sigma_{\text{h3-1}}$, defined as the standard deviation under the pure-recurrent configuration divided by that under the highest attention ratio (25\%). Values greater than 1 indicate that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.
55	
56	\subsection{Per-Example Decomposition}\label{ssec:per_example_decomposition}
57	
58	We decompose performance changes at the individual-example level on four benchmarks spanning three task categories: LAMBADA (5{,}153 examples; retrieval), HellaSwag (10{,}042; reasoning), PIQA (1{,}838; reasoning, two-choice), and ARC-Easy (2{,}376; mixed). HellaSwag uses normalized accuracy (acc\_norm) as the per-example correctness criterion. Supplementary analyses on BoolQ (3{,}270) and WinoGrande (1{,}267) appear in Appendix~\ref{app:boolq} and \ref{app:winogrande}. For each architecture, we compare the pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid (25\% attention), which maximizes the recurrent--hybrid contrast; \S\ref{ssec:per_example} confirms that $\alpha$ is stable across intermediate ratios (variation $< 0.03$). Each example is classified as:
59	
60	\begin{itemize}
61	    \item \textbf{Helped}: correct in the hybrid but incorrect in the pure model.
62	    \item \textbf{Hurt}: correct in the pure model but incorrect in the hybrid.
63	    \item \textbf{Unchanged}: both models agree (both correct or both incorrect).
64	\end{itemize}
65	
66	The net effect for each architecture is $|\text{helped}| - |\text{hurt}|$. A positive net effect means more examples transition to correct than to incorrect when attention is added; a negative net effect means the opposite.
67	
68	To measure cross-architecture overlap, we compute the pairwise Jaccard similarity $J = |A \cap B| / |A \cup B|$ between the helped sets of two architectures and, separately, between their hurt sets. $J = 1$ indicates identical sets; $J = 0$ indicates no overlap. On $K$-choice tasks, binarization collapses $K{-}1$ wrong answers into a single ``incorrect'' category, potentially counting architectures as agreeing (both wrong) when they select different wrong answers. This conservative bias strengthens the multiplicity claim: true divergence may exceed what binary Jaccard captures. Appendix~\ref{app:continuous_jaccard} confirms robustness under continuous Jaccard similarity.
69	
70	\subsection{Statistical Methods}\label{ssec:statistical_methods}
71	
72	All tests are two-tailed. Spearman $\rho$ uses exact permutation tests ($5! = 120$ permutations); with $n = 5$, only $|\rho| = 1.0$ achieves $p < .05$ ($p = .017$), so all Spearman-based inferences are descriptive and exploratory. Bonferroni correction spans all 10 benchmarks (adjusted threshold $\alpha / 10 = 0.005$). Bootstrap 95\% bias-corrected and accelerated (BCa) confidence intervals use 10{,}000 resamples; with $n = 3$ architectures, these are heuristic indicators of effect direction. Within-architecture comparisons use Mann-Whitney U; cross-architecture comparisons use Kruskal-Wallis. Attention weight analysis extracts per-layer cosine similarity and Shannon entropy via forward hooks (Appendix~\ref{app:attention_patterns}). The permutation null does not condition on item difficulty; if difficulty-driven correlations inflate observed Jaccard while permutation destroys this structure, the test may be anti-conservative. However, the ${\sim}2.5\times$ observed-to-null ratio (all $z > 5.9$) provides substantial margin against this bias.
73	
```

- LAMBADA: `\citep{paperno2016lambada}` ✅ (line 33)


- ARC-Challenge: `\citealt{clark2018arc}` ✅ (line 35)
- lm-evaluation-harness: `\citep{gao2024lmeval}` ✅ (line 41)
- Zoology: `\citep{arora2024zoology}` ✅ (line 30)

But I'm noticing several benchmark datasets that lack citations: BoolQ, HellaSwag, WinoGrande, PIQA, and COPA all appear around lines 34-35 without proper references.

Looking further, ARC-Easy and ARC-Challenge both come from the same paper, but only ARC-Challenge has the citation. Then there's MMLU, SciQ, and XStoryCloze on line 36 that also need citations. In the experiments section, Pythia 70M is mentioned but only cited in the appendix, not inline. I also need to add bibliography entries for GLA and DeltaNet since they're referenced in the introduction but lack proper citations.

Now I'm going through the benchmarks to identify which ones need new bib entries. HellaSwag, PIQA, GLA, and DeltaNet are already handled, but I still need to add entries for BoolQ, WinoGrande, and COPA with their proper author citations and publication details.

Looking back at the method section, I notice the benchmarks are listed by name but several aren't actually cited in the bibliography yet—HellaSwag, PIQA, WinoGrande, COPA, and BoolQ are all missing, and ARC-Easy isn't separately cited from ARC-Challenge. I need to track down the exact citations for MMLU, SciQ, and XStoryCloze as well.

Since ARC-Easy comes from the same paper as ARC-Challenge, I can reuse that citation. Now I'll add all the missing bibliography entries and insert the citations where each benchmark is first mentioned in the method section.

    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe describe the model configurations, evaluation benchmarks, and analytical framework used to measure how attention affects cross-architecture agreement at both aggregate and per-example levels.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Model zoo summary \\citep{wang2025hybrid}. All configurations share training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters; they differ only in backbone and attention ratio.}\n\\label{tab:model_zoo}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}\n\\toprule\nArchitecture & Recurrence mechanism & Scales \\\\\n\\midrule\nGatedDeltaNet & Gated delta rules & 340M, 1.3B \\\\\nRetNet & Multi-scale exp.\\ decay & 340M, 1.3B \\\\\nHGRN2 & Gated state expansion & 340M, 1.3B \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l@{}}{\\footnotesize Attention ratios: 0\\%, 4\\%, 7.7\\%, 14.3\\%, 25\\%} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l@{}}{\\footnotesize Extended (340M only): +GLA, +DeltaNet} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Model Zoo}\\label{ssec:model_zoo}\n\nWe use the pretrained model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid} (Table~\\ref{tab:model_zoo}), which provides controlled comparisons: GatedDeltaNet \\citep{yang2024gateddeltanet}, RetNet \\citep{sun2023retnet}, and HGRN2 \\citep{qin2024hgrn2} at five attention ratios and two scales. The GDN 1.3B h3-1 configuration uses 16 attention heads vs.\\ 32 for RetNet and HGRN2 (\\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}). We adopt the h$k$-1 notation: h3-1 = 25\\% attention, h6-1 = 14.3\\%, h12-1 = 7.7\\%, h24-1 = 4\\%. Within each scale, all models share identical training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters, isolating architecture and attention configuration. The zoo also includes a Transformer baseline at both scales.\n\n\\subsection{Task Taxonomy}\\label{ssec:task_taxonomy}\n\nWe evaluate on 10 benchmarks grouped into four categories defined by the primary cognitive demand of each task. This taxonomy (informal; not pre-registered) was fixed prior to analysis following standard categorizations in the evaluation literature \\citep{arora2024zoology} and is treated as hypothesis-generating rather than confirmatory (see Appendix~\\ref{app:benchmark_details} for the confirmatory/exploratory split), not derived post hoc from observed results. The categories test whether the effect of attention on cross-architecture variance depends on task type.\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Retrieval}: LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada}, which requires predicting a final word from long-range context without reasoning or world knowledge.\n    \\item \\textbf{Comprehension}: Boolean Questions (BoolQ), a reading comprehension task with yes/no answers.\n    \\item \\textbf{Reasoning}: HellaSwag, AI2 Reasoning Challenge (ARC-Challenge; \\citealt{clark2018arc}), WinoGrande, and Physical Intuition Question Answering (PIQA). Choice of Plausible Alternatives (COPA; $n = 100$) is supplementary; its small sample renders $\\alpha$ unreliable (Appendix~\\ref{app:subsample_sensitivity}).\n    \\item \\textbf{Mixed}: ARC-Easy, Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU), SciQ, and XStoryCloze, which combine retrieval, comprehension, and reasoning demands.\n\\end{itemize}\n\nLAMBADA is the only free-form generation benchmark in our evaluation; all others use multiple-choice scoring. Its higher $\\alpha$ (0.30 vs.\\ 0.14--0.21 for multiple-choice tasks) may partly reflect the larger answer space inherent to open-ended prediction, and the multiple-choice format may mechanically constrain per-example divergence by limiting the number of distinct error patterns. We cannot fully disentangle task type from evaluation format with the present benchmarks. However, within multiple-choice tasks, format (two-way vs.\\ four-way) does not predict $\\alpha$ (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}), suggesting that format alone does not drive the observed variation.\n\nAll evaluations use lm-evaluation-harness v0.4.x \\citep{gao2024lmeval} with default prompts and greedy decoding; accuracy serves as the primary metric for all benchmarks. Bootstrap analyses use random seed 42 throughout.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_method_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Experimental framework. For each architecture, we compare the pure-recurrent baseline against its hybrid (+attention) variant, classifying every evaluation example as helped, hurt, or unchanged. Cross-architecture overlap of these per-example effect sets is measured via pairwise Jaccard similarity ($J \\approx 0.10$), revealing architecture-specific sensitivity patterns.}\n\\label{fig:method_overview}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Convergence Metrics}\\label{ssec:convergence_metrics}\n\nWe quantify cross-architecture agreement as a function of attention ratio. For each benchmark and attention ratio, we compute the cross-architecture standard deviation $\\sigma$ over the three architectures' accuracy scores. To measure the relationship between attention and convergence, we compute the Spearman rank correlation $\\rho$ between attention ratio rank and $\\sigma$ across the five attention configurations ($n = 5$). A negative $\\rho$ indicates that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.\n\nWe also report the convergence ratio $\\sigma_{\\text{pure}} / \\sigma_{\\text{h3-1}}$, defined as the standard deviation under the pure-recurrent configuration divided by that under the highest attention ratio (25\\%). Values greater than 1 indicate that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Example Decomposition}\\label{ssec:per_example_decomposition}\n\nWe decompose performance changes at the individual-example level on four benchmarks spanning three task categories: LAMBADA (5{,}153 examples; retrieval), HellaSwag (10{,}042; reasoning), PIQA (1{,}838; reasoning, two-choice), and ARC-Easy (2{,}376; mixed). HellaSwag uses normalized accuracy (acc\\_norm) as the per-example correctness criterion. Supplementary analyses on BoolQ (3{,}270) and WinoGrande (1{,}267) appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq} and \\ref{app:winogrande}. For each architecture, we compare the pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid (25\\% attention), which maximizes the recurrent--hybrid contrast; \\S\\ref{ssec:per_example} confirms that $\\alpha$ is stable across intermediate ratios (variation $< 0.03$). Each example is classified as:\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Helped}: correct in the hybrid but incorrect in the pure model.\n    \\item \\textbf{Hurt}: correct in the pure model but incorrect in the hybrid.\n    \\item \\textbf{Unchanged}: both models agree (both correct or both incorrect).\n\\end{itemize}\n\nThe net effect for each architecture is $|\\text{helped}| - |\\text{hurt}|$. A positive net effect means more examples transition to correct than to incorrect when attention is added; a negative net effect means the opposite.\n\nTo measure cross-architecture overlap, we compute the pairwise Jaccard similarity $J = |A \\cap B| / |A \\cup B|$ between the helped sets of two architectures and, separately, between their hurt sets. $J = 1$ indicates identical sets; $J = 0$ indicates no overlap. On $K$-choice tasks, binarization collapses $K{-}1$ wrong answers into a single ``incorrect'' category, potentially counting architectures as agreeing (both wrong) when they select different wrong answers. This conservative bias strengthens the multiplicity claim: true divergence may exceed what binary Jaccard captures. Appendix~\\ref{app:continuous_jaccard} confirms robustness under continuous Jaccard similarity.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Methods}\\label{ssec:statistical_methods}\n\nAll tests are two-tailed. Spearman $\\rho$ uses exact permutation tests ($5! = 120$ permutations); with $n = 5$, only $|\\rho| = 1.0$ achieves $p < .05$ ($p = .017$), so all Spearman-based inferences are descriptive and exploratory. Bonferroni correction spans all 10 benchmarks (adjusted threshold $\\alpha / 10 = 0.005$). Bootstrap 95\\% bias-corrected and accelerated (BCa) confidence intervals use 10{,}000 resamples; with $n = 3$ architectures, these are heuristic indicators of effect direction. Within-architecture comparisons use Mann-Whitney U; cross-architecture comparisons use Kruskal-Wallis. Attention weight analysis extracts per-layer cosine similarity and Shannon entropy via forward hooks (Appendix~\\ref{app:attention_patterns}). The permutation null does not condition on item difficulty; if difficulty-driven correlations inflate observed Jaccard while permutation destroys this structure, the test may be anti-conservative. However, the ${\\sim}2.5\\times$ observed-to-null ratio (all $z > 5.9$) provides substantial margin against this bias.\n",

99	This pattern generalizes beyond LAMBADA (Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling}c): on all four main benchmarks, mean pairwise Jaccard shows no significant trend with attention ratio (all $\rho \leq 0.80$, $p \geq 0.333$), with values uniformly between 0.08 and 0.11 (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_bench_jaccard}).
100	
101	Heuristic bootstrap 95\% CIs ($n = 3$; \S\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}) show non-overlapping $\sigma$ between pure and h3-1 but completely overlapping Jaccard across all hybrid ratios (\S\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}). Figure~\ref{fig:jaccard_hierarchy} shows a three-tier Jaccard hierarchy: independence baseline (${\sim}0.05$), cross-architecture (${\sim}0.10$, $2\times$ baseline), and within-architecture (${\sim}0.33$, $3\times$ cross-architecture; Appendix~\ref{app:within_arch_stability}). Pythia 70M seed calibration yields a comparable within-seed level ($J = 0.27$--$0.39$; Appendix~\ref{app:seed_calibration}), consistent with architecture-associated divergence, though matched multi-seed experiments would be needed for definitive evidence.
102	
103	\begin{figure}[t]

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01QLb6DnxEHRjzRvuXw1cujZ`</summary>

```text
28	Our contributions are as follows:
29	\begin{itemize}
30	    \item Architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models, controlled within a single model zoo sharing training data and hyperparameters, produces structured predictive multiplicity whose magnitude varies by task type (or evaluation format) rather than attention ratio (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}). Five-architecture validation confirms the pattern (\S\ref{ssec:robustness}; Appendix~\ref{app:extended_arch}).
31	    \item Per-example overlap appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\%) across all four benchmarks: minimal attention may establish which examples each architecture affects, with no observed homogenization at higher ratios ($n = 5$ ratios; \S\ref{ssec:decoupling}).
32	    \item Pure-model confidence margins partially predict which examples attention will affect (AUC 0.70--0.74), with rescue thresholds architecture-invariant but disruption thresholds varying 47\% across architectures, constituting a second dimension of multiplicity beyond per-example disagreement (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}; Appendix~\ref{app:prediction_framework}).
33	\end{itemize}
34	\noindent Code and data are available at \url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/entrainment_gated_ft}.
```

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "3f4380e5-6934-42e6-98ff-174e7dfc7d6f",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "3ba761f8-4110-4ee6-a45d-600ecf758c29",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "d472fad4-2c16-43f1-9a1b-d8397e41986a",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "Hybrid linear recurrent architectures (GatedDeltaNet \\citealp{yang2024gateddeltanet}, RetNet \\citealp{sun2023retnet}, HGRN2 \\citealp{qin2024hgrn2}, GLA \\citealp{yang2024gla}, DeltaNet \\citealp{yang2024deltanet}) achieve aggregate benchmark scores within 1--2 points of each other",
    "newString": "Hybrid linear recurrent architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2, GLA, DeltaNet) achieve aggregate benchmark scores within 1--2 points of each other",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHybrid linear recurrent architectures (GatedDeltaNet \\citealp{yang2024gateddeltanet}, RetNet \\citealp{sun2023retnet}, HGRN2 \\citealp{qin2024hgrn2}, GLA \\citealp{yang2024gla}, DeltaNet \\citealp{yang2024deltanet}) achieve aggregate benchmark scores within 1--2 points of each other at matched parameter budgets, yet their helped and hurt example sets overlap by only ${\\sim}10$\\% (pairwise Jaccard $\\approx 0.10$). This gap between aggregate equivalence and per-example behavioral equivalence is invisible in standard metrics: architectures that score similarly reach that score by gaining and losing on different inputs, and the choice of backbone carries hidden consequences for which specific examples a deployed system handles correctly.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.56\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}%\n\\hfill\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.42\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig4_jaccard_saturation.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}\n\\caption{Per-example overlap appears stable from minimal attention across benchmarks. (a,\\,b) LAMBADA: cross-architecture $\\sigma$ shows a non-significant decreasing trend at 1.3B (solid; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed), while pairwise Jaccard of helped/hurt sets appears stable from 4\\% attention onward. (c) This pattern holds across all four main benchmarks: mean pairwise $\\bar{J}$ remains flat across attention ratios (all $\\rho \\leq 0.80$, $p \\geq .333$). Shaded bands are illustrative ($n = 3$).}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nPrior work on predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} and underspecification \\citep{damour2022underspecification,mccoy2020berts} documents this risk for seed variation within a single architecture, but whether it extends to architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models remains open. If architectures that score similarly in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, the backbone choice carries hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first controlled within-family per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0--25\\%) and two scales (340M, 1.3B). We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed categories. On four benchmarks (LAMBADA \\citealp{paperno2016lambada}, $N = 5{,}153$; HellaSwag \\citealp{zellers2019hellaswag}, $N = 10{,}042$; PIQA \\citealp{bisk2020piqa}, $N = 1{,}838$; ARC-Easy \\citealp{clark2018arc}, $N = 2{,}376$) we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap via Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central finding: architectures that appear equivalent by aggregate benchmarks diverge systematically at the per-example level, and this divergence is established by minimal attention.\n\nOur central finding is that architectural variation produces structured predictive multiplicity whose magnitude depends on task type, not attention ratio. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), varies $2\\times$ across task types but only 0.03 within each benchmark across attention ratios; pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across all ten five-architecture pairs). A supporting observation is that per-example overlap appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat at higher ratios. Even on LAMBADA, where aggregate variance shows a non-significant decreasing trend ($\\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; at 1.3B only), per-example Jaccard does not change (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}).\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item Architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models, controlled within a single model zoo sharing training data and hyperparameters, produces structured predictive multiplicity whose magnitude varies by task type (or evaluation format) rather than attention ratio (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}).     "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHybrid linear recurrent architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2, GLA, DeltaNet) achieve aggregate benchmark scores within 1--2 points of each other at matched parameter budgets, yet their helped and hurt example sets overlap by only ${\\sim}10$\\% (pairwise Jaccard $\\approx 0.10$). This gap between aggregate equivalence and per-example behavioral equivalence is invisible in standard metrics: architectures that score similarly reach that score by gaining and losing on different inputs, and the choice of backbone carries hidden consequences for which specific examples a deployed system handles correctly.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.56\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}%\n\\hfill\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.42\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig4_jaccard_saturation.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}\n\\caption{Per-example overlap appears stable from minimal attention across benchmarks. (a,\\,b) LAMBADA: cross-architecture $\\sigma$ shows a non-significant decreasing trend at 1.3B (solid; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed), while pairwise Jaccard of helped/hurt sets appears stable from 4\\% attention onward. (c) This pattern holds across all four main benchmarks: mean pairwise $\\bar{J}$ remains flat across attention ratios (all $\\rho \\leq 0.80$, $p \\geq .333$). Shaded bands are illustrative ($n = 3$).}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nPrior work on predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} and underspecification \\citep{damour2022underspecification,mccoy2020berts} documents this risk for seed variation within a single architecture, but whether it extends to architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models remains open. If architectures that score similarly in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, the backbone choice carries hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first controlled within-family per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0--25\\%) and two scales (340M, 1.3B). We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed categories. On four benchmarks (LAMBADA \\citealp{paperno2016lambada}, $N = 5{,}153$; HellaSwag \\citealp{zellers2019hellaswag}, $N = 10{,}042$; PIQA \\citealp{bisk2020piqa}, $N = 1{,}838$; ARC-Easy \\citealp{clark2018arc}, $N = 2{,}376$) we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap via Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central finding: architectures that appear equivalent by aggregate benchmarks diverge systematically at the per-example level, and this divergence is established by minimal attention.\n\nOur central finding is that architectural variation produces structured predictive multiplicity whose magnitude depends on task type, not attention ratio. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), varies $2\\times$ across task types but only 0.03 within each benchmark across attention ratios; pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across all ten five-architecture pairs). A supporting observation is that per-example overlap appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat at higher ratios. Even on LAMBADA, where aggregate variance shows a non-significant decreasing trend ($\\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; at 1.3B only), per-example Jaccard does not change (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}).\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item Architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models, controlled within a single model zoo sharing training data and hyperparameters, produces structured predictive multiplicity whose magnitude varies by task type (or evaluation format) rather than attention ratio (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}). Five-architecture validation confirms the pattern (\\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}; Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_arch}).\n    \\item Per-example overlap appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\\%) across all four benchmarks: minimal attention may establish which examples each architecture affects, with no observed homogenization at higher ratios ($n = 5$ ratios; \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}).\n    \\item Pure-model confidence margins partially predict which examples attention will affect (AUC 0.70--0.74), with rescue thresholds architecture-invariant but disruption thresholds varying 47\\% across architectures, constituting a second dimension of multiplicity beyond per-example disagreement (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}; Appendix~\\ref{app:prediction_framework}).\n\\end{itemize}\n\\noindent Code and data are available at \\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/entrainment_gated_ft}.\n",

```text
1	\vspace{-2pt}
2	\section{Conclusion}
3	
4	We have shown that hybrid linear recurrent architectures which appear interchangeable by aggregate benchmarks diverge substantially at the per-example level. This multiplicity varies by task type ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) rather than attention ratio. Per-example overlap appears to stabilize at 4\% attention (pairwise Jaccard $\leq 0.10$); two interpretations (architecture-specific fingerprints or rapid convergence toward Transformer-like behavior) remain open (Appendix~\ref{app:transformer_entrainment}). Confidence margins partially predict which examples are affected (AUC 0.70--0.74), with rescue thresholds architecture-invariant but disruption thresholds varying 47\%.
5	
6	At the 340M--1.3B scales studied here, per-example audit before architecture substitution is advisable when multiplicity is substantial; aggregate equivalence does not guarantee behavioral equivalence. Low cross-architecture Jaccard creates complementarity ($+7$--$14$\,pp oracle gain), though preliminary routing experiments show exploitation remains task-dependent ($+5.3$\,pp on LAMBADA; $-0.6$\,pp on PIQA).
7	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013yXU3zd7gvjWJNqnz1H3GM`</summary>

```text
45	Each architecture displays a distinct profile of per-example changes (Figure~\ref{fig:per_example_bars}). GDN loses a net 153 examples (462 helped, 615 hurt), RetNet loses 69 (426/495), and HGRN2 gains 153 (548/395; full counts in Appendix~\ref{app:per_example_counts}). HGRN2 is the only architecture for which adding attention yields a net per-example benefit. Pairwise Jaccard similarity is low: mean $\bar{J} \leq 0.10$, indicating ${\sim}90$\% non-overlap. Overlap significantly exceeds the independence baseline ($J_{\text{null}} \approx 0.05$, all $z > 5.9$, $p < .0001$; Appendix~\ref{app:jaccard_permutation}), plausibly reflecting shared sensitivity to example difficulty. Normalizing by $J_{\max}$, observed overlap reaches only 6--7\% of the achievable range above chance (Appendix~\ref{app:normalized_es}), confirming that low Jaccard cannot be attributed to set-size constraints.
46	
47	\begin{figure}[t]
48	\centering
49	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_alpha_routing.pdf}
50	\caption{(a) Per-example ambiguity $\alpha$ varies $2\times$ across task types. (b) Confidence-based routing gains track $\alpha$: effective when multiplicity is high (LAMBADA) but counterproductive when low (PIQA). 1.3B, h3-1.}
51	\label{fig:alpha_routing}
52	\end{figure}
53	
54	\paragraph{Confidence margin predictor.} Pure-model confidence on the target token predicts which examples will be affected (AUC 0.70--0.74, a non-parametric measure equivalent to Mann-Whitney $U$; Appendix~\ref{app:confidence_margin}): rescue thresholds are architecture-invariant (1\% spread) but disruption thresholds vary 47\%, explaining cross-architecture Jaccard divergence (Appendix~\ref{app:prediction_framework}). Calibration differences account for less than half the threshold spread (Appendix~\ref{app:calibration}). The rescue pattern generalizes across three benchmarks ($d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\ref{app:cm_cross}); disruption is weaker on reasoning tasks (Appendix~\ref{app:boolq}). This structured disagreement constitutes predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive} under architectural variation.
55	
56	\paragraph{Confidence-based routing.} As an illustrative case study, the rescue-threshold asymmetry suggests a routing strategy: select the architecture whose pure-model confidence best predicts successful rescue. On LAMBADA, this outperforms majority vote by $+5.3$\,pp (SD $= 0.4$\,pp, 10 cross-validated splits), extracting 36\% of the oracle gap (oracle = 65.5\%; Appendix~\ref{app:complementarity}). On ARC-Easy, gains are modest ($+1.0$\,pp, SD $= 0.5$\,pp); on HellaSwag, routing provides no benefit ($-0.02$\,pp, $p = .89$); on PIQA, it is significantly negative ($-0.6$\,pp, $p = .006$). Routing works when multiplicity is high ($\alpha = 0.30$, LAMBADA) and a clear rescue-threshold signal exists, but is counterproductive when multiplicity is low ($\alpha = 0.14$, PIQA). A natural comparison (average-logits ensemble) is left to future work as it requires simultaneous inference across all architectures.
57	
58	\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\alpha$ \citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy; Appendix~\ref{app:arceasy}) to 0.30 (LAMBADA). Format does not drive this ordering: HellaSwag (four-way) and PIQA (two-choice) produce nearly identical $\alpha$ despite different answer-space sizes, and $\alpha$ does not correlate with task accuracy ($\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). This within-multiple-choice invariance suggests that per-example multiplicity is associated with the cognitive demands of the task rather than the mechanical constraints of the evaluation format, though the confound between task type and format cannot be fully resolved with the present benchmarks (Limitations). Bootstrap 95\% CIs confirm the ordering (all non-overlapping except PIQA--HellaSwag; Table~\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\bar{J} \leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many are affected. Within each benchmark, $\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$) but differs $2\times$ across task types (or evaluation formats), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention ratio \citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}.
59	
60	\begin{table}[t]
61	\centering
62	\small
63	\caption{Predictive multiplicity at 1.3B. $\alpha$: ambiguity at h3-1 \citep{marx2020predictive}. $\bar{J}$: mean pairwise Jaccard of helped~(h) and hurt~(u) sets, averaged across four hybrid attention ratios and three architecture pairs. $\rho_\alpha$: Spearman $\rho$ between attention ratio and $\alpha$. Benchmarks ordered by $\alpha$.}
64	\label{tab:multiplicity}
65	\begin{tabular}{@{}lrcccc@{}}
66	\toprule
67	 & $N$ & $\alpha$ & $\bar{J}_{h}$ & $\bar{J}_{u}$ & $\rho_\alpha$ \\
68	\midrule
69	PIQA & 1838 & .14 & .091 & .094 & $0.0$ \\
70	HellaSwag & 10042 & .15 & .095 & .085 & $0.1$ \\
71	ARC-Easy & 2376 & .21 & .092 & .099 & $-0.2$ \\
72	LAMBADA & 5153 & .30 & .096 & .091 & $-0.6$ \\
73	\bottomrule
74	\end{tabular}
75	\par\vspace{-2pt}
76	{\footnotesize Within-benchmark $\alpha$ variation $< 0.03$ across ratios. BoolQ, WinoGrande: Appendix~\ref{app:boolq}, \ref{app:winogrande}.}
77	\end{table}
78	
79	\subsection{Per-Example Overlap Appears Stable from Minimal Attention}\label{ssec:decoupling}
80	
81	Having established that multiplicity varies by task type, we now ask whether it changes with the amount of attention. Does adding more attention change \emph{which} examples each architecture affects? Pairwise Jaccard appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\%; $\bar{J} \approx 0.095$) and remains flat across all higher ratios (Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling}b), suggesting that minimal attention may suffice to establish the per-example divergence pattern, though the small number of ratios ($n = 5$) limits statistical confirmation. As background, LAMBADA's aggregate cross-architecture $\sigma$ shows a non-significant decreasing trend ($\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; excluding GDN: $\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$; MMLU reverses to $\rho = +0.6$ when excluding GDN; observed at 1.3B, absent at 340M; Table~\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\ref{fig:task_specificity}). No other benchmark shows a comparable aggregate pattern ($n = 5$; negligible power for $|\rho| < 1.0$; Appendix~\ref{app:convergence_detail}).
82	
83	\begin{figure}[t]
84	\centering
85	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_task_specificity.pdf}
86	\caption{Spearman $\rho$ between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\sigma$ for each benchmark at 1.3B scale. LAMBADA ($\rho = -1.0$; BCa 95\% CI $[-1.0,\,-0.7]$, heuristic) is the only benchmark with a negative correlation whose confidence interval excludes zero. Benchmarks are grouped by task category. Bootstrap CI is heuristic given $n = 3$ architectures.}
87	\label{fig:task_specificity}
88	\end{figure}
89	
90	\begin{figure*}[t]
91	\centering
92	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/jaccard_heatmap_5arch.pdf}
93	\caption{Pairwise Jaccard heatmap across five architectures (340M, h3-1), where helped (hurt) examples are those correct only with (without) attention. Upper triangle: $J_{\text{helped}}$; lower triangle: $J_{\text{hurt}}$. All values $\leq 0.14$, all permutation tests $p \leq .002$. HellaSwag and PIQA results in Appendix~\ref{app:extended_arch}.}
94	\label{fig:jaccard_heatmap_5arch}
95	\end{figure*}
96	
97	Each architecture's per-example response to attention is largely idiosyncratic. The observed $\bar{J}$ reaches only 6--7\% of the achievable range above chance ($J_{\max} \approx 0.78$--$0.92$), ruling out ceiling constraints (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}). Readers can verify $\rho$ directly from Table~\ref{tab:convergence}; with $n = 3$ architectures, all bootstrap CIs are heuristic indicators of direction rather than precise coverage.
98	
99	This pattern generalizes beyond LAMBADA (Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling}c): on all four main benchmarks, mean pairwise Jaccard shows no significant trend with attention ratio (all $\rho \leq 0.80$, $p \geq 0.333$), with values uniformly between 0.08 and 0.11 (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_bench_jaccard}).
```
