Normalizing by $J_{\\max}$, observed overlap reaches only 6--7\\% of the achievable range above chance (Appendix~\\ref{app:normalized_es}), confirming that low Jaccard cannot be attributed to set-size constraints.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_alpha_routing.pdf}\n\\caption{(a) Per-example ambiguity $\\alpha$ varies $2\\times$ across task types. (b) Confidence-based routing gains track $\\alpha$: effective when multiplicity is high (LAMBADA) but counterproductive when low (PIQA). 1.3B, h3-1.}\n\\label{fig:alpha_routing}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Confidence margin predictor.} Pure-model confidence on the target token predicts which examples will be affected (AUC 0.70--0.74, a non-parametric measure equivalent to Mann-Whitney $U$; Appendix~\\ref{app:confidence_margin}): rescue thresholds are architecture-invariant (1\\% spread) but disruption thresholds vary 47\\%, explaining cross-architecture Jaccard divergence (Appendix~\\ref{app:prediction_framework}). Calibration differences account for less than half the threshold spread (Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}). The rescue pattern generalizes across three benchmarks ($d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cm_cross}); disruption is weaker on reasoning tasks (Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}). This structured disagreement constitutes predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} under architectural variation.\n\n\\paragraph{Confidence-based routing.} As an illustrative case study, the rescue-threshold asymmetry suggests a routing strategy: select the architecture whose pure-model confidence best predicts successful rescue. On LAMBADA, this outperforms majority vote by $+5.3$\\,pp (SD $= 0.4$\\,pp, 10 cross-validated splits), extracting 36\\% of the oracle gap (oracle = 65.5\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:complementarity}). On ARC-Easy, gains are modest ($+1.0$\\,pp, SD $= 0.5$\\,pp); on HellaSwag, routing provides no benefit ($-0.02$\\,pp, $p = .89$); on PIQA, it is significantly negative ($-0.6$\\,pp, $p = .006$). Routing works when multiplicity is high ($\\alpha = 0.30$, LAMBADA) and a clear rescue-threshold signal exists, but is counterproductive when multiplicity is low ($\\alpha = 0.14$, PIQA). A natural comparison (average-logits ensemble) is left to future work as it requires simultaneous inference across all architectures.\n\n\\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy; Appendix~\\ref{app:arceasy}) to 0.30 (LAMBADA). Format does not drive this ordering: HellaSwag (four-way) and PIQA (two-choice) produce nearly identical $\\alpha$ despite different answer-space sizes, and $\\alpha$ does not correlate with task accuracy ($\\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). This within-multiple-choice invariance suggests that per-example multiplicity is associated with the cognitive demands of the task rather than the mechanical constraints of the evaluation format, though the confound between task type and format cannot be fully resolved with the present benchmarks (Limitations). Bootstrap 95\\% CIs confirm the ordering (all non-overlapping except PIQA--HellaSwag; Table~\\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\bar{J} \\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many are affected. Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$) but differs $2\\times$ across task types (or evaluation formats), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention ratio \\citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Predictive multiplicity at 1.3B. $\\alpha$: ambiguity at h3-1 \\citep{marx2020predictive}. $\\bar{J}$: mean pairwise Jaccard of helped~(h) and hurt~(u) sets, averaged across four hybrid attention ratios and three architecture pairs. $\\rho_\\alpha$: Spearman $\\rho$ between attention ratio and $\\alpha$. Benchmarks ordered by $\\alpha$.}\n\\label{tab:multiplicity}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n & $N$ & $\\alpha$ & $\\bar{J}_{h}$ & $\\bar{J}_{u}$ & $\\rho_\\alpha$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPIQA & 1838 & .14 & .091 & .094 & $0.0$ \\\\\nHellaSwag & 10042 & .15 & .095 & .085 & $0.1$ \\\\\nARC-Easy & 2376 & .21 & .092 & .099 & $-0.2$ \\\\\nLAMBADA & 5153 & .30 & .096 & .091 & $-0.6$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\par\\vspace{-2pt}\n{\\footnotesize Within-benchmark $\\alpha$ variation $< 0.03$ across ratios. BoolQ, WinoGrande: Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}, \\ref{app:winogrande}.}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Per-Example Overlap Appears Stable from Minimal Attention}\\label{ssec:decoupling}\n\nHaving established that multiplicity varies by task type, we now ask whether it changes with the amount of attention. Does adding more attention change \\emph{which} examples each architecture affects? Pairwise Jaccard appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\\%; $\\bar{J} \\approx 0.095$) and remains flat across all higher ratios (Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling}b), suggesting that minimal attention may suffice to establish the per-example divergence pattern, though the small number of ratios ($n = 5$) limits statistical confirmation. As background, LAMBADA's aggregate cross-architecture $\\sigma$ shows a non-significant decreasing trend ($\\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; excluding GDN: $\\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$; MMLU reverses to $\\rho = +0.6$ when excluding GDN; observed at 1.3B, absent at 340M; Table~\\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\\ref{fig:task_specificity}). No other benchmark shows a comparable aggregate pattern ($n = 5$; negligible power for $|\\rho| < 1.0$; Appendix~\\ref{app:convergence_detail}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_task_specificity.pdf}\n\\caption{Spearman $\\rho$ between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\\sigma$ for each benchmark at 1.3B scale. LAMBADA ($\\rho = -1.0$; BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$, heuristic) is the only benchmark with a negative correlation whose confidence interval excludes zero. Benchmarks are grouped by task category. Bootstrap CI is heuristic given $n = 3$ architectures.}\n\\label{fig:task_specificity}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/jaccard_heatmap_5arch.pdf}\n\\caption{Pairwise Jaccard heatmap across five architectures (340M, h3-1), where helped (hurt) examples are those correct only with (without) attention. Upper triangle: $J_{\\text{helped}}$; lower triangle: $J_{\\text{hurt}}$. All values $\\leq 0.14$, all permutation tests $p \\leq .002$. HellaSwag and PIQA results in Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_arch}.}\n\\label{fig:jaccard_heatmap_5arch}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nEach architecture's per-example response to attention is largely idiosyncratic. The observed $\\bar{J}$ reaches only 6--7\\% of the achievable range above chance ($J_{\\max} \\approx 0.78$--$0.92$), ruling out ceiling constraints (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}). Readers can verify $\\rho$ directly from Table~\\ref{tab:convergence}; with $n = 3$ architectures, all bootstrap CIs are heuristic indicators of direction rather than precise coverage.\n\nThis pattern generalizes beyond LAMBADA (Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling}c): on all four main benchmarks, mean pairwise Jaccard shows no significant trend with attention ratio (all $\\rho \\leq 0.80$, $p \\geq 0.333$), with values uniformly between 0.08 and 0.11 (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_bench_jaccard}).",

3	Using the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid} (\S\ref{ssec:model_zoo}), we evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks plus supplementary COPA ($n = 100$) at 1.3B scale unless noted. Results at 340M appear in scale analyses. Recall: h$k$-1 denotes one attention layer per $k{+}1$ layers; h3-1 = 25\% attention.
4	
5	\begin{figure}[t]
6	\centering
7	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_per_example_bars.pdf}
8	\caption{Per-example effects of adding attention (pure $\to$ h3-1) on LAMBADA at 1.3B scale. Helped: correct only in the hybrid; hurt: correct only in the pure model. HGRN2 is the only architecture with a net positive effect. Despite comparable magnitudes, these sets overlap minimally across architectures ($\bar{J} \leq 0.10$, all $p < .0001$).}
9	\label{fig:per_example_bars}
10	\end{figure}
11	
12	\begin{table*}[t]
13	\centering
14	\caption{Cross-architecture variance trends across benchmarks (1.3B scale). $\rho$: Spearman correlation between attention ratio and cross-architecture standard deviation $\sigma$ across five configurations. $p$: exact permutation test (two-tailed, 120 permutations). $\sigma$ values reported $\times 10^{2}$ (accuracy percentage points). Ratio: $\sigma_{\text{pure}} / \sigma_{\text{h3-1}}$. \textbf{Bold}: only benchmark with $\rho = -1.0$. HellaSwag evaluated using normalized accuracy (acc\_norm), the standard metric for this benchmark.}

          " ",
          " Each architecture displays a distinct profile of per-example changes (Figure~\\ref{fig:per_example_bars}). GDN loses a net 153 examples (462 helped, 615 hurt), RetNet loses 69 (426/495), and HGRN2 gains 153 (548/395; full counts in Appendix~\\ref{app:per_example_counts}). HGRN2 is the only architecture for which adding attention yields a net per-example benefit. Pairwise Jaccard similarity is low: mean $\\bar{J} \\leq 0.10$, indicating ${\\sim}90$\\% non-overlap. Overlap significantly exceeds the independence baseline ($J_{\\text{null}} \\approx 0.05$, all $z > 5.9$, $p < .0001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:jaccard_permutation}), plausibly reflecting shared sensitivity to example difficulty. Normalizing by $J_{\\max}$, observed overlap reaches only 6--7\\% of the achievable range above chance (Appendix~\\ref{app:normalized_es}), confirming that low Jaccard cannot be attributed to set-size constraints.",
          " ",
          "-\\begin{figure}[t]",
          "-\\centering",
          "-\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_alpha_routing.pdf}",
          "-\\caption{(a) Per-example ambiguity $\\alpha$ varies $2\\times$ across task types. (b) Confidence-based routing gains track $\\alpha$: effective when multiplicity is high (LAMBADA) but counterproductive when low (PIQA). 1.3B, h3-1.}",
          "-\\label{fig:alpha_routing}",
          "-\\end{figure}",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Confidence margin predictor.} Pure-model confidence on the target token predicts which examples will be affected (AUC 0.70--0.74, a non-parametric measure equivalent to Mann-Whitney $U$; Appendix~\\ref{app:confidence_margin}): rescue thresholds are architecture-invariant (1\\% spread) but disruption thresholds vary 47\\%, explaining cross-architecture Jaccard divergence (Appendix~\\ref{app:prediction_framework}). Calibration differences account for less than half the threshold spread (Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}). The rescue pattern generalizes across three benchmarks ($d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cm_cross}); disruption is weaker on reasoning tasks (Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}). This structured disagreement constitutes predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} under architectural variation.",
          " "
        ]
      }

```text
Looking at page 5: The figures are no longer visible at all. The text fills both columns without the figures. This means the `figure*[t]` floated elsewhere.

Looking at page 8: I can see at the top of page 8, there are two figures side by side spanning the full width - these are Figure 3 (per-example bars) on the left and Figure 4 (alpha routing) on the right. Below them are Figure 6 (Jaccard hierarchy) on the left and some other figure on the right. Then there's "4.3 Robustness" and "5 Conclusion" sections.


Actually, I'm seeing the figures more clearly now - the top of page 8 has Figure 6 (the Jaccard heatmap) on the left with bar charts on the right, followed by more side-by-side figures below. The problem is that Figures 3 and 4 were supposed to stay on page 5, but they've floated all the way to page 8 instead, which breaks the document flow. The issue is that `figure*[t]` placement in two-column mode is limited to the top of pages, and they're getting pushed too far ahead.

I need to rethink this. The core problem is that `figure*` floats defined in the source only appear at the top of the next available page, and since page 5 has dense text, LaTeX is pushing them forward. I could try placing the figure definition earlier in the source, or use `\suppressfloats` with `[!t]`, or add `\FloatBarrier` to constrain placement. The real issue is that in two-column mode, `figure*` can only go at page tops, so wherever it's defined in the experiments section, it'll appear on the following page. I'm realizing the issue: the `table*` placement right after the `figure*` is creating a conflict for top-of-page slots, with LaTeX processing them sequentially and potentially blocking the figure from appearing where intended. I should try moving the combined figure before the section heading or use stronger placement directives like `[t!]` or `[!htbp]` to give it priority, since the original single-column figures appeared in the right column of page 5 but `figure*` requires full-width spanning which changes the layout dynamics. I need to check where the figure* environment is currently positioned in section 4 - if it's not at the very beginning, moving it there with the `[!t]` placement specifier should force it to the top of the page and resolve the layout issue.
```

```text
1	\section{Experiments}\label{sec:experiments}
2	
3	Using the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid} (\S\ref{ssec:model_zoo}), we evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks plus supplementary COPA ($n = 100$) at 1.3B scale unless noted. Results at 340M appear in scale analyses. Recall: h$k$-1 denotes one attention layer per $k{+}1$ layers; h3-1 = 25\% attention.
4	
5	\begin{figure*}[t]
6	\centering
7	\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\textwidth}
8	\centering
9	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig_per_example_bars.pdf}
10	\caption{Per-example effects of adding attention (pure $\to$ h3-1) on LAMBADA at 1.3B scale. Helped: correct only in the hybrid; hurt: correct only in the pure model. HGRN2 is the only architecture with a net positive effect. Despite comparable magnitudes, these sets overlap minimally across architectures ($\bar{J} \leq 0.10$, all $p < .0001$).}
11	\label{fig:per_example_bars}
12	\end{minipage}%
13	\hfill
14	\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\textwidth}
15	\centering
16	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig3_alpha_routing.pdf}
17	\caption{(a) Per-example ambiguity $\alpha$ varies $2\times$ across task types. (b) Confidence-based routing gains track $\alpha$: effective when multiplicity is high (LAMBADA) but counterproductive when low (PIQA). 1.3B, h3-1.}
18	\label{fig:alpha_routing}
19	\end{minipage}
20	\end{figure*}
21	
22	\begin{table*}[t]
23	\centering
24	\caption{Cross-architecture variance trends across benchmarks (1.3B scale). $\rho$: Spearman correlation between attention ratio and cross-architecture standard deviation $\sigma$ across five configurations. $p$: exact permutation test (two-tailed, 120 permutations). $\sigma$ values reported $\times 10^{2}$ (accuracy percentage points). Ratio: $\sigma_{\text{pure}} / \sigma_{\text{h3-1}}$. \textbf{Bold}: only benchmark with $\rho = -1.0$. HellaSwag evaluated using normalized accuracy (acc\_norm), the standard metric for this benchmark.}
25	\label{tab:convergence}
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The issue is that both `figure*[t]` and `table*[t]` are defined at the beginning of §4. In two-column LaTeX, `figure*` and `table*` can only appear at the top (or bottom with special packages) of pages. They float to the next page. Since both are defined near the start of §4, which itself starts on a new page, both will try to float to the same or subsequent pages.

The `figure*` at line 5 will try to go to the top of the next page after where it's processed. The `table*` at line 22 will go to the page after that.


I need to figure out where §4 actually starts in the page layout—the method section takes up about two pages, so §4 should begin around page 5. If I move the `figure*` to the end of method.tex, it should float to the top of the page where §4 begins, which would help with the spacing issue.
```

_source line 1210 · 2026-05-25T09:47:14.084Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01TocaB7sbiEDDAyseenvvae`</summary>

```text
65	
66	The net effect for each architecture is $|\text{helped}| - |\text{hurt}|$. A positive net effect means more examples transition to correct than to incorrect when attention is added; a negative net effect means the opposite.
67	
68	To measure cross-architecture overlap, we compute the pairwise Jaccard similarity $J = |A \cap B| / |A \cup B|$ between the helped sets of two architectures and, separately, between their hurt sets. $J = 1$ indicates identical sets; $J = 0$ indicates no overlap. On $K$-choice tasks, binarization collapses $K{-}1$ wrong answers into a single ``incorrect'' category, potentially counting architectures as agreeing (both wrong) when they select different wrong answers. This conservative bias strengthens the multiplicity claim: true divergence may exceed what binary Jaccard captures. Appendix~\ref{app:continuous_jaccard} confirms robustness under continuous Jaccard similarity.
69	
70	\subsection{Statistical Methods}\label{ssec:statistical_methods}
71	
72	All tests are two-tailed. Spearman $\rho$ uses exact permutation tests ($5! = 120$ permutations); with $n = 5$, only $|\rho| = 1.0$ achieves $p < .05$ ($p = .017$), so all Spearman-based inferences are descriptive and exploratory. Bonferroni correction spans all 10 benchmarks (adjusted threshold $\alpha / 10 = 0.005$). Bootstrap 95\% bias-corrected and accelerated (BCa) confidence intervals use 10{,}000 resamples; with $n = 3$ architectures, these are heuristic indicators of effect direction. Within-architecture comparisons use Mann-Whitney U; cross-architecture comparisons use Kruskal-Wallis. Attention weight analysis extracts per-layer cosine similarity and Shannon entropy via forward hooks (Appendix~\ref{app:attention_patterns}). The permutation null does not condition on item difficulty; if difficulty-driven correlations inflate observed Jaccard while permutation destroys this structure, the test may be anti-conservative. However, the ${\sim}2.5\times$ observed-to-null ratio (all $z > 5.9$) provides substantial margin against this bias.
73	
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe describe the model configurations, evaluation benchmarks, and analytical framework used to measure how attention affects cross-architecture agreement at both aggregate and per-example levels.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Model zoo summary \\citep{wang2025hybrid}. All configurations share training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters; they differ only in backbone and attention ratio.}\n\\label{tab:model_zoo}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}\n\\toprule\nArchitecture & Recurrence mechanism & Scales \\\\\n\\midrule\nGatedDeltaNet & Gated delta rules & 340M, 1.3B \\\\\nRetNet & Multi-scale exp.\\ decay & 340M, 1.3B \\\\\nHGRN2 & Gated state expansion & 340M, 1.3B \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l@{}}{\\footnotesize Attention ratios: 0\\%, 4\\%, 7.7\\%, 14.3\\%, 25\\%} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l@{}}{\\footnotesize Extended (340M only): +GLA, +DeltaNet} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Model Zoo}\\label{ssec:model_zoo}\n\nWe use the pretrained model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid} (Table~\\ref{tab:model_zoo}), which provides controlled comparisons: GatedDeltaNet \\citep{yang2024gateddeltanet}, RetNet \\citep{sun2023retnet}, and HGRN2 \\citep{qin2024hgrn2} at five attention ratios and two scales. The GDN 1.3B h3-1 configuration uses 16 attention heads vs.\\ 32 for RetNet and HGRN2 (\\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}). We adopt the h$k$-1 notation: h3-1 = 25\\% attention, h6-1 = 14.3\\%, h12-1 = 7.7\\%, h24-1 = 4\\%. Within each scale, all models share identical training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters, isolating architecture and attention configuration. The zoo also includes a Transformer baseline at both scales.\n\n\\subsection{Task Taxonomy}\\label{ssec:task_taxonomy}\n\nWe evaluate on 10 benchmarks grouped into four categories defined by the primary cognitive demand of each task. This taxonomy (informal; not pre-registered) was fixed prior to analysis following standard categorizations in the evaluation literature \\citep{arora2024zoology} and is treated as hypothesis-generating rather than confirmatory (see Appendix~\\ref{app:benchmark_details} for the confirmatory/exploratory split), not derived post hoc from observed results. The categories test whether the effect of attention on cross-architecture variance depends on task type.\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Retrieval}: LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada}, which requires predicting a final word from long-range context without reasoning or world knowledge.\n    \\item \\textbf{Comprehension}: BoolQ \\citep{clark2019boolq}, a reading comprehension task with yes/no answers.\n    \\item \\textbf{Reasoning}: HellaSwag \\citep{zellers2019hellaswag}, ARC-Challenge \\citep{clark2018arc}, WinoGrande \\citep{sakaguchi2021winogrande}, and PIQA \\citep{bisk2020piqa}. COPA \\citep{roemmele2011copa} ($n = 100$) is supplementary; its small sample renders $\\alpha$ unreliable (Appendix~\\ref{app:subsample_sensitivity}).\n    \\item \\textbf{Mixed}: ARC-Easy \\citep{clark2018arc}, MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}, SciQ \\citep{welbl2017sciq}, and XStoryCloze \\citep{lin2022xstorycloze}, which combine retrieval, comprehension, and reasoning demands.\n\\end{itemize}\n\nLAMBADA is the only free-form generation benchmark in our evaluation; all others use multiple-choice scoring. Its higher $\\alpha$ (0.30 vs.\\ 0.14--0.21 for multiple-choice tasks) may partly reflect the larger answer space inherent to open-ended prediction, and the multiple-choice format may mechanically constrain per-example divergence by limiting the number of distinct error patterns. We cannot fully disentangle task type from evaluation format with the present benchmarks. However, within multiple-choice tasks, format (two-way vs.\\ four-way) does not predict $\\alpha$ (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}), suggesting that format alone does not drive the observed variation.\n\nAll evaluations use lm-evaluation-harness v0.4.x \\citep{gao2024lmeval} with default prompts and greedy decoding; accuracy serves as the primary metric for all benchmarks. Bootstrap analyses use random seed 42 throughout.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_method_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Experimental framework. For each architecture, we compare the pure-recurrent baseline against its hybrid (+attention) variant, classifying every evaluation example as helped, hurt, or unchanged. Cross-architecture overlap of these per-example effect sets is measured via pairwise Jaccard similarity ($J \\approx 0.10$), revealing architecture-specific sensitivity patterns.}\n\\label{fig:method_overview}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Convergence Metrics}\\label{ssec:convergence_metrics}\n\nWe quantify cross-architecture agreement as a function of attention ratio. For each benchmark and attention ratio, we compute the cross-architecture standard deviation $\\sigma$ over the three architectures' accuracy scores. To measure the relationship between attention and convergence, we compute the Spearman rank correlation $\\rho$ between attention ratio rank and $\\sigma$ across the five attention configurations ($n = 5$). A negative $\\rho$ indicates that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.\n\nWe also report the convergence ratio $\\sigma_{\\text{pure}} / \\sigma_{\\text{h3-1}}$, defined as the standard deviation under the pure-recurrent configuration divided by that under the highest attention ratio (25\\%). Values greater than 1 indicate that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Example Decomposition}\\label{ssec:per_example_decomposition}\n\nWe decompose performance changes at the individual-example level on four benchmarks spanning three task categories: LAMBADA (5{,}153 examples; retrieval), HellaSwag (10{,}042; reasoning), PIQA (1{,}838; reasoning, two-choice), and ARC-Easy (2{,}376; mixed). HellaSwag uses normalized accuracy (acc\\_norm) as the per-example correctness criterion. Supplementary analyses on BoolQ (3{,}270) and WinoGrande (1{,}267) appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq} and \\ref{app:winogrande}. For each architecture, we compare the pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid (25\\% attention), which maximizes the recurrent--hybrid contrast; \\S\\ref{ssec:per_example} confirms that $\\alpha$ is stable across intermediate ratios (variation $< 0.03$). Each example is classified as:\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Helped}: correct in the hybrid but incorrect in the pure model.\n    \\item \\textbf{Hurt}: correct in the pure model but incorrect in the hybrid.\n    \\item \\textbf{Unchanged}: both models agree (both correct or both incorrect).\n\\end{itemize}\n\nThe net effect for each architecture is $|\\text{helped}| - |\\text{hurt}|$. A positive net effect means more examples transition to correct than to incorrect when attention is added; a negative net effect means the opposite.\n\nTo measure cross-architecture overlap, we compute the pairwise Jaccard similarity $J = |A \\cap B| / |A \\cup B|$ between the helped sets of two architectures and, separately, between their hurt sets. $J = 1$ indicates identical sets; $J = 0$ indicates no overlap. On $K$-choice tasks, binarization collapses $K{-}1$ wrong answers into a single ``incorrect'' category, potentially counting architectures as agreeing (both wrong) when they select different wrong answers. This conservative bias strengthens the multiplicity claim: true divergence may exceed what binary Jaccard captures. Appendix~\\ref{app:continuous_jaccard} confirms robustness under continuous Jaccard similarity.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Methods}\\label{ssec:statistical_methods}\n\nAll tests are two-tailed. Spearman $\\rho$ uses exact permutation tests ($5! = 120$ permutations); with $n = 5$, only $|\\rho| = 1.0$ achieves $p < .05$ ($p = .017$), so all Spearman-based inferences are descriptive and exploratory. Bonferroni correction spans all 10 benchmarks (adjusted threshold $\\alpha / 10 = 0.005$). Bootstrap 95\\% bias-corrected and accelerated (BCa) confidence intervals use 10{,}000 resamples; with $n = 3$ architectures, these are heuristic indicators of effect direction. Within-architecture comparisons use Mann-Whitney U; cross-architecture comparisons use Kruskal-Wallis. Attention weight analysis extracts per-layer cosine similarity and Shannon entropy via forward hooks (Appendix~\\ref{app:attention_patterns}). The permutation null does not condition on item difficulty; if difficulty-driven correlations inflate observed Jaccard while permutation destroys this structure, the test may be anti-conservative. However, the ${\\sim}2.5\\times$ observed-to-null ratio (all $z > 5.9$) provides substantial margin against this bias.\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_0149i3sGVxVUPSEFqX3wuGEV`</summary>

```text
43	\paragraph{Confidence-based routing.} As an illustrative case study, the rescue-threshold asymmetry suggests a routing strategy: select the architecture whose pure-model confidence best predicts successful rescue. On LAMBADA, this outperforms majority vote by $+5.3$\,pp (SD $= 0.4$\,pp, 10 cross-validated splits), extracting 36\% of the oracle gap (oracle = 65.5\%; Appendix~\ref{app:complementarity}). On ARC-Easy, gains are modest ($+1.0$\,pp, SD $= 0.5$\,pp); on HellaSwag, routing provides no benefit ($-0.02$\,pp, $p = .89$); on PIQA, it is significantly negative ($-0.6$\,pp, $p = .006$). Routing works when multiplicity is high ($\alpha = 0.30$, LAMBADA) and a clear rescue-threshold signal exists, but is counterproductive when multiplicity is low ($\alpha = 0.14$, PIQA). A natural comparison (average-logits ensemble) is left to future work as it requires simultaneous inference across all architectures.
44	
45	\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\alpha$ \citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy; Appendix~\ref{app:arceasy}) to 0.30 (LAMBADA). Format does not drive this ordering: HellaSwag (four-way) and PIQA (two-choice) produce nearly identical $\alpha$ despite different answer-space sizes, and $\alpha$ does not correlate with task accuracy ($\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). This within-multiple-choice invariance suggests that per-example multiplicity is associated with the cognitive demands of the task rather than the mechanical constraints of the evaluation format, though the confound between task type and format cannot be fully resolved with the present benchmarks (Limitations). Bootstrap 95\% CIs confirm the ordering (all non-overlapping except PIQA--HellaSwag; Table~\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\bar{J} \leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many are affected. Within each benchmark, $\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$) but differs $2\times$ across task types (or evaluation formats), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention ratio \citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}.
46	
47	\begin{table}[t]
48	\centering
49	\small
50	\caption{Predictive multiplicity at 1.3B. $\alpha$: ambiguity at h3-1 \citep{marx2020predictive}. $\bar{J}$: mean pairwise Jaccard of helped~(h) and hurt~(u) sets, averaged across four hybrid attention ratios and three architecture pairs. $\rho_\alpha$: Spearman $\rho$ between attention ratio and $\alpha$. Benchmarks ordered by $\alpha$.}
51	\label{tab:multiplicity}
52	\begin{tabular}{@{}lrcccc@{}}
53	\toprule
54	 & $N$ & $\alpha$ & $\bar{J}_{h}$ & $\bar{J}_{u}$ & $\rho_\alpha$ \\
55	\midrule
56	PIQA & 1838 & .14 & .091 & .094 & $0.0$ \\
57	HellaSwag & 10042 & .15 & .095 & .085 & $0.1$ \\
58	ARC-Easy & 2376 & .21 & .092 & .099 & $-0.2$ \\
59	LAMBADA & 5153 & .30 & .096 & .091 & $-0.6$ \\
60	\bottomrule
61	\end{tabular}
62	\par\vspace{-2pt}
63	{\footnotesize Within-benchmark $\alpha$ variation $< 0.03$ across ratios. BoolQ, WinoGrande: Appendix~\ref{app:boolq}, \ref{app:winogrande}.}
64	\end{table}
65	
66	\subsection{Per-Example Overlap Appears Stable from Minimal Attention}\label{ssec:decoupling}
67

Routing works when multiplicity is high ($\\alpha = 0.30$, LAMBADA) and a clear rescue-threshold signal exists, but is counterproductive when multiplicity is low ($\\alpha = 0.14$, PIQA). A natural comparison (average-logits ensemble) is left to future work as it requires simultaneous inference across all architectures.\n\n\\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy; Appendix~\\ref{app:arceasy}) to 0.30 (LAMBADA). Format does not drive this ordering: HellaSwag (four-way) and PIQA (two-choice) produce nearly identical $\\alpha$ despite different answer-space sizes, and $\\alpha$ does not correlate with task accuracy ($\\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). This within-multiple-choice invariance suggests that per-example multiplicity is associated with the cognitive demands of the task rather than the mechanical constraints of the evaluation format, though the confound between task type and format cannot be fully resolved with the present benchmarks (Limitations). Bootstrap 95\\% CIs confirm the ordering (all non-overlapping except PIQA--HellaSwag; Table~\\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\bar{J} \\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many are affected. Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$) but differs $2\\times$ across task types (or evaluation formats), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention ratio \\citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Predictive multiplicity at 1.3B. $\\alpha$: ambiguity at h3-1 \\citep{marx2020predictive}. $\\bar{J}$: mean pairwise Jaccard of helped~(h) and hurt~(u) sets, averaged across four hybrid attention ratios and three architecture pairs. $\\rho_\\alpha$: Spearman $\\rho$ between attention ratio and $\\alpha$. Benchmarks ordered by $\\alpha$.}\n\\label{tab:multiplicity}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n & $N$ & $\\alpha$ & $\\bar{J}_{h}$ & $\\bar{J}_{u}$ & $\\rho_\\alpha$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPIQA & 1838 & .14 & .091 & .094 & $0.0$ \\\\\nHellaSwag & 10042 & .15 & .095 & .085 & $0.1$ \\\\\nARC-Easy & 2376 & .21 & .092 & .099 & $-0.2$ \\\\\nLAMBADA & 5153 & .30 & .096 & .091 & $-0.6$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\par\\vspace{-2pt}\n{\\footnotesize Within-benchmark $\\alpha$ variation $< 0.03$ across ratios. BoolQ, WinoGrande: Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}, \\ref{app:winogrande}.}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Per-Example Overlap Appears Stable from Minimal Attention}\\label{ssec:decoupling}\n",
      "numLines": 25,
      "startLine": 43,
      "totalLines": 104
    }

33	
34	\subsection{Per-Example Divergence}\label{ssec:per_example}
35	
36	Do architectures converge by improving on the same examples? We compare each architecture's pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid on 5{,}153 LAMBADA examples, classifying each as helped, hurt, or unchanged. Although the decomposition is binary, it captures genuine separation in the continuous confidence space (Cohen's $d = 0.73$--$0.92$ between helped and hurt on LAMBADA; cross-benchmark details in Appendix~\ref{app:cm_cross}). Binarization discards confidence magnitude, so we supplement with continuous confidence margin analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:confidence_margin}) and verify that low Jaccard persists under continuous thresholds (Appendix~\ref{app:continuous_jaccard}).
37	
38	Each architecture displays a distinct profile of per-example changes (Figure~\ref{fig:per_example_bars}). GDN loses a net 153 examples (462 helped, 615 hurt), RetNet loses 69 (426/495), and HGRN2 gains 153 (548/395; full counts in Appendix~\ref{app:per_example_counts}). HGRN2 is the only architecture for which adding attention yields a net per-example benefit. Pairwise Jaccard similarity is low: mean $\bar{J} \leq 0.10$, indicating ${\sim}90$\% non-overlap. Overlap significantly exceeds the independence baseline ($J_{\text{null}} \approx 0.05$, all $z > 5.9$, $p < .0001$; Appendix~\ref{app:jaccard_permutation}), plausibly reflecting shared sensitivity to example difficulty. Normalizing by $J_{\max}$, observed overlap reaches only 6--7\% of the achievable range above chance (Appendix~\ref{app:normalized_es}), confirming that low Jaccard cannot be attributed to set-size constraints.
39	
40	
41	\paragraph{Confidence margin predictor.} Pure-model confidence on the target token predicts which examples will be affected (AUC 0.70--0.74, a non-parametric measure equivalent to Mann-Whitney $U$; Appendix~\ref{app:confidence_margin}): rescue thresholds are architecture-invariant (1\% spread) but disruption thresholds vary 47\%, explaining cross-architecture Jaccard divergence (Appendix~\ref{app:prediction_framework}). Calibration differences account for less than half the threshold spread (Appendix~\ref{app:calibration}). The rescue pattern generalizes across three benchmarks ($d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\ref{app:cm_cross}); disruption is weaker on reasoning tasks (Appendix~\ref{app:boolq}). This structured disagreement constitutes predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive} under architectural variation.
42

  "content": "[飞书用户 用户]: ⚠️ 紧急修正：匿名仓库脚注格式问题\n\n之前发的匿名仓库链接规范有表述不清的地方，导致很多论文的 Abstract 最后一句话被截断或改写了。请立刻按以下标准修正。\n\n---\n\n问题描述：\n\n很多论文现在的 Abstract 最后一句话写到一半就断了，或者被改成了不完整的句子。这是因为之前的模板让你把 \"We release our code and data at...\" 直接拼在了 Abstract 原文里，破坏了原来的句子结构。\n\n正确做法：\n\n1. Abstract 原文保持不动 —— 不要删改、截断、替换 Abstract 的任何一句话\n2. 在 Abstract 最后一句话的句号之前加一个 \\footnote{}，脚注标记（上标 ¹）挂在句末\n3. 整句 \"Code and data are available at ...\" 写在脚注里面，不要出现在 Abstract 正文中\n\n标准 LaTeX 模板：\n\n... (Abstract 原来的最后一句话，一个字都不要改)\\footnote{Code and data are available at \\url{你的匿名仓库地址}.}.\n\n具体示例：\n\n假设你的 Abstract 最后一句原文是 \"Our experiments demonstrate significant improvements across all benchmarks.\"\n\n✅ 正确：\n\nOur experiments demonstrate significant improvements across all benchmarks\\footnote{Code and data are available at \\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/your-project}.}.\n\n❌ 错误（之前很多人的做法）：\n\nOur experiments demonstrate significant improvements. We release our code and data at\\footnote{\\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/your-project}}.\n\n❌ 错误（截断原句）：\n\nOur experiments\\footnote{Code and data are available at \\url{...}}.\n\n---\n\n请立刻检查并修复：\n\n1. 打开你的论文 Abstract 部分\n2. 确认最后一句话是完整的、未被修改的原始句子\n3. 如果句子被截断或改写了，恢复原文\n4. 按上面的模板把脚注挂在最后一句的句号前\n\n完成后请汇报：Abstract 最后一句话是什么？脚注格式是否已按上述标准修正？"
}

_source line 1302 · 2026-05-25T10:04:51.601Z_

[飞书用户 用户]: ⚠️ 紧急修正：匿名仓库脚注格式问题

之前发的匿名仓库链接规范有表述不清的地方，导致很多论文的 Abstract 最后一句话被截断或改写了。请立刻按以下标准修正。

---

问题描述：

很多论文现在的 Abstract 最后一句话写到一半就断了，或者被改成了不完整的句子。这是因为之前的模板让你把 "We release our code and data at..." 直接拼在了 Abstract 原文里，破坏了原来的句子结构。

正确做法：

1. Abstract 原文保持不动 —— 不要删改、截断、替换 Abstract 的任何一句话
2. 在 Abstract 最后一句话的句号之前加一个 \footnote{}，脚注标记（上标 ¹）挂在句末
3. 整句 "Code and data are available at ..." 写在脚注里面，不要出现在 Abstract 正文中

标准 LaTeX 模板：

... (Abstract 原来的最后一句话，一个字都不要改)\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{你的匿名仓库地址}.}.

具体示例：

假设你的 Abstract 最后一句原文是 "Our experiments demonstrate significant improvements across all benchmarks."

✅ 正确：

Our experiments demonstrate significant improvements across all benchmarks\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/your-project}.}.

❌ 错误（之前很多人的做法）：

Our experiments demonstrate significant improvements. We release our code and data at\footnote{\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/your-project}}.

❌ 错误（截断原句）：

Our experiments\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{...}}.

---

请立刻检查并修复：

1. 打开你的论文 Abstract 部分
2. 确认最后一句话是完整的、未被修改的原始句子
3. 如果句子被截断或改写了，恢复原文
4. 按上面的模板把脚注挂在最后一句的句号前

完成后请汇报：Abstract 最后一句话是什么？脚注格式是否已按上述标准修正？

36	\maketitle
37	
38	\begin{abstract}
39	Hybrid linear recurrent architectures that appear interchangeable by aggregate benchmarks can differ on which specific examples they handle correctly. Using a controlled model zoo \citep{wang2025hybrid} (three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales), we decompose per-example effects of adding attention and find structured predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive}: pairwise overlap of helped and hurt example sets is uniformly low (Jaccard $\approx 0.10$, roughly twice the independence baseline; all $p < .01$ across five architectures). This multiplicity varies by task type or evaluation format ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) rather than attention ratio. Per-example overlap appears to stabilize at 4\% attention and remains flat across higher ratios, suggesting that minimal attention may establish which examples each architecture affects. \citet{yang2026benchmark} document analogous per-item disagreement (16--66\%) among frontier large language models (LLMs) despite comparable aggregate scores; our within-family design isolates backbone variation from training-regime differences. Confidence margins partially predict affected examples (area under the ROC curve [AUC] 0.70--0.74). Low cross-architecture overlap implies substantial complementarity, though routing-based exploitation remains task-dependent.
40	\end{abstract}

Per-example overlap appears to stabilize at 4\\% attention and remains flat across higher ratios, suggesting that minimal attention may establish which examples each architecture affects. \\citet{yang2026benchmark} document analogous per-item disagreement (16--66\\%) among frontier large language models (LLMs) despite comparable aggregate scores; our within-family design isolates backbone variation from training-regime differences. Confidence margins partially predict affected examples (area under the ROC curve [AUC] 0.70--0.74). Low cross-architecture overlap implies substantial complementarity, though routing-based exploitation remains task-dependent.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{sections/introduction}\n\n\\input{sections/related_work}\n\n\\input{sections/method}\n\n\\input{sections/experiments}\n\n\\input{sections/conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Single training runs.} Each configuration is a single training run; consistency across all 40 architecture--benchmark combinations (all $p < .01$) and a $3\\times$ within- vs.\\ cross-architecture Jaccard gap (within-seed $J$ is 2.7--4.3$\\times$ higher; Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_calibration}) mitigate seed confounds but cannot fully resolve them.\n\n\\paragraph{Format confound.} The confound between task type and evaluation format cannot be fully resolved: LAMBADA is the only free-form benchmark, so its higher $\\alpha$ may reflect format differences rather than cognitive demands alone.\n\n\\paragraph{Coverage.} We examine three primary architectures (plus two extended) from one model zoo, four main benchmarks, and two scale points. Generalization to Mamba, RWKV, other hybrid families, and generation tasks remains open; all four per-example benchmarks use discriminative scoring, so multiplicity under open-ended generation is untested. Results at larger scales (e.g., 7B+) may differ, particularly as attention mechanisms become more expressive with increased capacity.\n\n\\paragraph{Pre-training only.} All evaluations use pretrained checkpoints without fine-tuning; per-example effects may not persist after task-specific adaptation. All evaluations use greedy decoding (the lm-eval-harness default); sampling strategies may alter per-example agreement patterns.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\\input{sections/appendix}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

This multiplicity varies by task type or evaluation format ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) rather than attention ratio. <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_019nmSaBeP5ud9QBGt34pdCn`</summary>

```text
28	Our contributions are as follows:
29	\begin{itemize}
30	    \item Architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models, controlled within a single model zoo sharing training data and hyperparameters, produces structured predictive multiplicity whose magnitude varies by task type (or evaluation format) rather than attention ratio (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}). Five-architecture validation confirms the pattern (\S\ref{ssec:robustness}; Appendix~\ref{app:extended_arch}).
31	    \item Per-example overlap appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\%) across all four benchmarks: minimal attention may establish which examples each architecture affects, with no observed homogenization at higher ratios ($n = 5$ ratios; \S\ref{ssec:decoupling}).
32	    \item Pure-model confidence margins partially predict which examples attention will affect (AUC 0.70--0.74), with rescue thresholds architecture-invariant but disruption thresholds varying 47\% across architectures, constituting a second dimension of multiplicity beyond per-example disagreement (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}; Appendix~\ref{app:prediction_framework}).
33	\end{itemize}
34	\noindent Code and data are available at \url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/entrainment_gated_ft}.
```

</details>

    "oldString": "\\end{itemize}\n\\noindent Code and data are available at \\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/entrainment_gated_ft}.",
    "newString": "\\end{itemize}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHybrid linear recurrent architectures \\citep[GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2, GLA, DeltaNet;][]{yang2024gateddeltanet,sun2023retnet,qin2024hgrn2,yang2024gla,yang2024deltanet} achieve aggregate benchmark scores within 1--2 points of each other at matched parameter budgets, yet their helped and hurt example sets overlap by only ${\\sim}10$\\% (pairwise Jaccard $\\approx 0.10$). This gap between aggregate equivalence and per-example behavioral equivalence is invisible in standard metrics: architectures that score similarly reach that score by gaining and losing on different inputs, and the choice of backbone carries hidden consequences for which specific examples a deployed system handles correctly.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.56\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}%\n\\hfill\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.42\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig4_jaccard_saturation.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}\n\\caption{Per-example overlap appears stable from minimal attention across benchmarks. (a,\\,b) LAMBADA: cross-architecture $\\sigma$ shows a non-significant decreasing trend at 1.3B (solid; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed), while pairwise Jaccard of helped/hurt sets appears stable from 4\\% attention onward. (c) This pattern holds across all four main benchmarks: mean pairwise $\\bar{J}$ remains flat across attention ratios (all $\\rho \\leq 0.80$, $p \\geq .333$). Shaded bands are illustrative ($n = 3$).}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nPrior work on predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} and underspecification \\citep{damour2022underspecification,mccoy2020berts} documents this risk for seed variation within a single architecture, but whether it extends to architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models remains open. If architectures that score similarly in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, the backbone choice carries hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first controlled within-family per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0--25\\%) and two scales (340M, 1.3B). We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed categories. On four benchmarks (LAMBADA \\citealp{paperno2016lambada}, $N = 5{,}153$; HellaSwag \\citealp{zellers2019hellaswag}, $N = 10{,}042$; PIQA \\citealp{bisk2020piqa}, $N = 1{,}838$; ARC-Easy \\citealp{clark2018arc}, $N = 2{,}376$) we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap via Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central finding: architectures that appear equivalent by aggregate benchmarks diverge systematically at the per-example level, and this divergence is established by minimal attention.\n\nOur central finding is that architectural variation produces structured predictive multiplicity whose magnitude depends on task type, not attention ratio. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), varies $2\\times$ across task types but only 0.03 within each benchmark across attention ratios; pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across all ten five-architecture pairs). A supporting observation is that per-example overlap appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat at higher ratios. Even on LAMBADA, where aggregate variance shows a non-significant decreasing trend ($\\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; at 1.3B only), per-example Jaccard does not change (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}).\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item Architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models, controlled within a single model zoo sharing training data and hyperparameters, produces structured predictive multiplicity whose magnitude varies by task type (or evaluation format) rather than attention ratio (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}). Five-architecture validation confirms the pattern (\\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}; Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_arch}).\n    \\item Per-example overlap appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\\%) across all four benchmarks: minimal attention may establish which examples each architecture affects, with no observed homogenization at higher ratios ($n = 5$ ratios; \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}).\n    \\item Pure-model confidence margins partially predict which examples attention will affect (AUC 0.70--0.74), with rescue thresholds architecture-invariant but disruption thresholds varying 47\\% across architectures, constituting a second dimension of multiplicity beyond per-example disagreement (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}; Appendix~\\ref{app:prediction_framework}).\n\\end{itemize}\n\\noindent Code and data are available at \\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/entrainment_gated_ft}.\n",
